### [Retatrutide Once Weekly for Treatment of Obstructive Sleep Apnea.](https://doi.org/10.1056/nejmoa2611477)

- Source: `nejm_abstracts` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `81` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42904878`

<h4>Background</h4>Retatrutide, an investigational triple agonist of glucose-dependent insulinotropic polypeptide, glucagon-like peptide 1, and glucagon receptors, may be useful for treating obstructive sleep apnea (OSA).<h4>Methods</h4>In two phase 3, double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled trials nested within larger basket trials of once-weekly subcutaneous retatrutide, we assigned adults with moderate-to-severe OSA and a body-mass index (the weight in kilograms divided by the square of the height in meters) of at least 27, without (TRIUMPH-1-OSA) or with (TRIUMPH-2-OSA) type 2 diabetes, to receive retatrutide (4, 9, or 12 mg) or placebo. The primary end point was the change in the apnea-hypopnea index (AHI, the average number of apneas and hypopneas per hour of sleep) at 80 weeks with 9 mg and 12 mg of retatrutide. For OSA-related end points, a hybrid-treatment estimand was applied, with a hypothetical strategy used for participants who had intercurrent events suggesting treatment failure and a treatment-policy strategy used elsewhere. We also report intention-to-treat (ITT) results.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 243 participants underwent randomization in TRIUMPH-1-OSA and 448 in TRIUMPH-2-OSA. The baseline mean AHI was 58.6 and 55.9 events per hour, respectively, and the mean body weight was 119.6 and 115.9 kg. At 80 weeks, the mean changes in the AHI (hybrid-treatment estimand) with retatrutide in TRIUMPH-1-OSA were -22.9 (4 mg), -34.3 (9 mg), and -31.7 (12 mg) events per hour, versus -9.9 with placebo (difference vs. placebo, -13.0 [95% confidence interval {CI}, -23.0 to -3.0], -24.4 [95% CI, -34.1 to -14.7], and -21.9 [95% CI, -32.2 to -11.5], respectively). The changes with retatrutide in TRIUMPH-2-OSA were -19.2 (4 mg), -24.7 (9 mg), and -25.4 (12 mg) events per hour, versus -10.3 with placebo (difference vs. placebo, -8.9 [95% CI, -15.9 to -2.0], -14.4 [95% CI, -21.6 to -7.2], and -15.1 [95% CI, -22.5 to -7.7]). For the ITT estimand, the corresponding differences from placebo were -13.2 (95% CI, -22.6 to -3.9), -24.7 (95% CI, -34.1 to -15.3), and -22.5 (95% CI, -31.9 to -13.1) in TRIUMPH-1-OSA and -8.8 (95% CI, -15.8 to -1.7), -13.9 (95% CI, -21.3 to -6.6), and -15.0 (95% CI, -22.5 to -7.4) in TRIUMPH-2-OSA. The mean percent change in body weight with retatrutide (key secondary end point) was -12.5 to -22.2% in TRIUMPH-1-OSA and -12.1 to -18.5% in TRIUMPH-2-OSA, versus -4.8% and -5.6% with placebo (treatment-regimen estimand). The most common adverse events were gastrointestinal.<h4>Conclusions</h4>In adults with OSA and obesity or overweight with or without type 2 diabetes, retatrutide resulted in significantly greater decreases in the AHI than placebo. (Funded by Eli Lilly; TRIUMPH-1 and -2 ClinicalTrials.gov numbers, NCT05929066 and NCT05929079.).

### [Disease-free survival and overall survival after neoadjuvant immunotherapy in mismatch repair-deficient colon cancer in the Netherlands: results of the single-arm, multicentre, phase 2, NICHE-2 trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01602-8)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `78` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42906564`

<h4>Background</h4>Neoadjuvant immune checkpoint blockade induces high pathological response rates in patients with locally advanced mismatch repair-deficient (dMMR) colon cancer, yet long-term survival outcomes have not been established. Previously, we reported safety (a primary endpoint) and pathological response (a secondary endpoint) data from the NICHE-2 trial. Here, we report the primary efficacy endpoint of 3-year disease-free survival and the exploratory endpoint of 5-year overall survival.<h4>Methods</h4>In this single-arm, multicentre, phase 2, NICHE-2 trial, patients with previously untreated, clinical stage 2 or 3, resectable dMMR colon cancer received a single dose of intravenous ipilimumab (1 mg per kg) on day 1, and two doses of intravenous nivolumab (3 mg per kg) on days 1 and 15, followed by surgery in one of the six participating hospitals in the Netherlands. Here, we report the independent primary efficacy endpoint of 3-year disease-free survival (time from surgery until disease recurrence or death from any cause) and the secondary endpoint of circulating tumour DNA (ctDNA) analysis. Overall survival (time from study enrolment until death from any cause) is reported as an exploratory post-hoc analysis. All endpoints were assessed in the per-protocol population (patients who met the eligibility criteria and received at least one cycle of therapy). The study is registered with ClinicalTrials.gov, NCT03026140, and has completed accrual.<h4>Findings</h4>From July 4, 2017, to July 18, 2022, 123 patients were screened, of whom 115 were treated and 111 were included in the per-protocol population. At the data cutoff (June 19, 2026), the postoperative median follow-up was 58·7 months (IQR 49·0-72·9). The 3-year disease-free survival was 100% (97·5% CI 100-100). After a median follow-up of 60·4 months (IQR 50·6-74·1), calculated from time of study enrolment, the 5-year overall survival was 100% (95% CI 100-100). In patients with available plasma samples, ctDNA was detected in 99 (92%) of 108 patients at baseline, and ctDNA clearance was observed in 44 (45%) of 98 patients after the first cycle in week 2 and in 80 (82%) of 97 patients after the second cycle before surgery. In patients with a pathological near-complete response and complete response, presurgery ctDNA clearance was observed in 19 (70%) of 27 patients and 60 (92%) of 65 patients, respectively. Assessment for minimal residual disease after surgery revealed that all patients were ctDNA negative.<h4>Interpretation</h4>In patients with locally advanced dMMR colon cancer, treatment with ipilimumab and nivolumab before surgery resulted in a 3-year disease-free survival and 5-year overall survival of 100%. These findings support neoadjuvant nivolumab plus ipilimumab as a potential standard of care for patients with locally advanced dMMR colon cancer.<h4>Funding</h4>Bristol Myers Squibb.

### [Relatlimab plus nivolumab versus nivolumab for advanced melanoma: 5-year survival and biomarker analyses of the phase 3 RELATIVITY-047 trial](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04756-1)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-10-24` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `78` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04756-1`

Nivolumab plus relatlimab is approved for advanced melanoma based on the RELATIVITY-047 trial; however, long-term data are needed. Here, we report data from RELATIVITY-047 with the longest follow-up at present. In this phase 2–3, double-blind trial, patients with metastatic/unresectable melanoma were randomized 1:1 to nivolumab 480 mg plus relatlimab 160 mg (n = 355) or nivolumab 480 mg (n = 359) intravenously every 4 weeks. The primary endpoint was progression-free survival (PFS) by blinded independent central review. Secondary endpoints were overall survival (OS), response, and safety. Additional post-hoc analyses such as melanoma-specific survival (MSS), and exploratory biomarker analyses are also reported. This analysis shows clear separation in survival curves with the largest absolute differences to date in PFS, OS, and MSS. In this prespecified 5-year analysis, with a minimum follow-up of 57.3 months, nivolumab plus relatlimab demonstrated continued clinically significant benefit versus nivolumab for PFS (hazard ratio = 0.77; 95% confidence interval: 0.65–0.92), OS (hazard ratio = 0.78; 95% confidence interval: 0.64–0.95), and MSS (hazard ratio = 0.71; 95% confidence interval: 0.57–0.88). The safety profile remained consistent with prior reports. Among biomarker-evaluable patients in the programmed death ligand 1 ≥1% subgroup, analyses revealed an association between worse outcomes with nivolumab and high peripheral or tumor lymphocyte activation gene 3 (LAG-3)+ CD8 cell levels. The addition of relatlimab to nivolumab was associated with a lower prevalence of primary resistance than nivolumab alone. These data support the hypothesis that relatlimab can overcome LAG-3–mediated resistance to nivolumab and provides evidence for differential efficacy patterns observed across biomarker subgroups linked to primary versus acquired resistance states. ClinicalTrials.gov identifier: NCT03470922. As presented at the ESMO Congress 2026: In a 5 year follow-up of survival and correlative data from the pivotal phase 3 RELATIVITY-047 trial comparing relatlimab plus nivolumab versus nivolumab alone in patients with metastatic or unresectable melanoma, statistically significant differences in progression-free survival, overall survival and melanoma-specific survival were seen with high baseline levels of LAG3 high CD8+ T cells associated with improved response in the combination arm.

### [A Common CD36 Variant and the Genetic Landscape of Dilated Cardiomyopathy in Individuals of African Ancestry.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18449)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `77` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42895716`

<h4>Importance</h4>Dilated cardiomyopathy (DCM) is a major cause of heart failure that disproportionately affects individuals of predominantly African ancestry, among whom familial clustering of disease is more pronounced relative to those of predominantly European ancestry. Truncating variants in TTN, which encodes the sarcomeric protein titin, are the most common established monogenic cause of DCM. A common CD36 nonsense variant enriched among individuals of African ancestry has also been shown to account for a substantial burden of DCM.<h4>Objective</h4>To compare the contribution of a CD36 nonsense variant to DCM risk with that of truncating variants in TTN and pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants in other established genes for DCM.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Multicohort genetic association study including participants of African and European ancestry with exome or genome sequence data and DCM case status from the following 4 US datasets: All of Us Research Program (recruitment: 2017-present; last follow-up: August 27, 2024), Million Veteran Program (recruitment: 2011-present; last follow-up: September 30, 2024), Penn Medicine Biobank (recruitment: 2013-present; last follow-up: November 14, 2022), and the DCM Precision Medicine Study (recruitment: 2016-2020; last follow-up: March 15, 2020).<h4>Exposure</h4>Carrier status for TTN truncating variants, pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants in 11 high-confidence genes for DCM, and the nonsense variant CD36 Y325* (0, 1, or 2 copies).<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Risk of DCM, prevalence of variant carriers among DCM cases, and population attributable fraction of DCM.<h4>Results</h4>Among 82 623 individuals of predominantly African ancestry across 4 cohorts, the mean age was 53.4 years (SD, 13.9 years), 47 124 were male, and 1316 had DCM. Individuals who were homozygous for the CD36 Y325* risk allele had a higher risk of DCM (odds ratio [OR], 4.8; 95% CI, 3.1-7.3) as did those who were heterozygous (OR, 1.4; 95% CI, 1.2-1.7). TTN truncating variants also conferred an elevated risk of DCM in participants of African ancestry (OR, 8.5; 95% CI, 5.5-13.0). Among 1316 individuals of African ancestry classified as DCM cases, 33 (2.5%) were homozygous for CD36, 56 (4.3%) had TTN truncating variants, and 16 (1.2%) had pathogenic or likely pathogenic variants in other high-confidence genes for DCM. In population-level analyses incorporating both heterozygous and homozygous CD36 Y325* carriers, the population attributable fraction for CD36 (9.0% [6.2% for heterozygous carriers and 2.8% for homozygous carriers]) surpassed the population attributable fraction for TTN truncating variants (3.7%).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In 4 US cohorts, an ancestry-specific CD36 variant contributed more to DCM burden among individuals of African ancestry than established genes for DCM, including TTN truncating variants that are typically considered the most common genetic cause. These findings reshape the known genetic architecture of DCM in individuals of African ancestry and highlight the importance of representation in genomic research.

### [Determinants of response to CAR T cell therapy in a multicenter, multi-trial cohort analysis of patients with lymphoma](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04707-w)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `77` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04707-w`

In patients with non-Hodgkin lymphoma (NHL) treated with chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T cell therapy, factors associated with efficacy overlap with prognostic biomarkers. Currently, there is no consensus on optimal product characteristics. We analyzed the phenotypic attributes of the anti-CD19 CAR T therapy axicabtagene ciloleucel (axi-cel) from 580 patients with NHL across multiple clinical trials with long-term follow-up (median, 41.9 months). A high proportion of naive-like (CCR7+CD45RA+) T cells was the most significant feature associated with prolonged progression-free survival (PFS; hazard ratio = 0.46, 95% confidence interval: 0.31−0.68, P = 9.8 × 10−5, high versus low naive-like T cell groups) without impacting safety. The relationship between naive-like T cells and efficacy was independent of tumor microenvironment characteristics (assessed in 112 patients). In multivariate models, the naive-like phenotype outperformed clinicopathologic features such as inflammatory markers and tumor burden, remaining significant after adjusting for product expansion. An inverse correlation was observed between prior lines of therapy and the proportion of naive-like T cells in both product and matched apheresis samples, suggesting a benefit of earlier intervention. The key findings were validated in an independent cohort of 203 patients treated with axi-cel-based therapy or brexucabtagene autoleucel. Mass cytometry by time-of-flight analysis of products from a subgroup of 116 patients confirmed an association between a CD8 naive-like T cell phenotype lacking expression of exhaustion markers, such as TIGIT, LAG-3 and PD-1, and PFS. The evidence generated by this study has the potential to advance the development of more effective cellular therapies. A large comprehensive analysis of clinical trial samples from patients with non-Hodgkin lymphoma who were treated with anti-CD19 CAR T therapy shows that a high proportion of naive-like and stem cell memory-like CCR7+CD45RA+ T cells in the pre-infusion CAR T product correlates with improved and prolonged clinical benefit, which was validated in an independent external cohort including different products.

### [Medicare Program; Implementation of Sections 6219, 6220, and 6224 of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 (CMS-4214-P)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-program-implementation-sections-6219-6220-6224-consolidated-appropriations-act-2026-cms)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-10-16` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `76` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-program-implementation-sections-6219-6220-6224-consolidated-appropriations-act-2026-cms`

Medicare Program; Implementation of Sections 6219, 6220, and 6224 of the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 (CMS-4214-P) Background On Oct. 16, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a proposed rule to implement key sections of the Consolidated Appropriations Act of 2026 including section 6220 (Requiring Enhanced and Accurate Lists of (REAL) Health Providers Act) and section 6224 (Modernizing and Ensuring Pharmacy Benefit Manager Accountability). This rule also proposes a conforming change mandated by section 6219 of the CAA (Adjustments to Medicare Part D Cost-Sharing Reductions for Low-Income Individuals) that would reduce the generic drug copayment from $1 to $0 for full-benefit dual eligible individuals. Implementing Certain Provisions of Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2026 On February 3, 2026, the CAA of 2026, which included several changes to the MA and Part D programs, was signed into law. In this rule, we are proposing to implement the following sections: Adjustments to Medicare Part D Cost-Sharing Reductions for Low-Income Individuals (Section 6219): This proposed rule implements Section 6219 of the CAA that would reduce the generic drug copayment from $1 to $0 for full-benefit dual eligible individuals at or below 100% of the Federal Poverty Level, effective for plan years starting Jan. 1, 2028. Requiring Enhanced and Accurate Lists of (REAL) Health Providers Act (Section 6220): CMS proposes to codify regulations to implement the REAL Health Providers Act, which establishes new provider directory verification and accuracy requirements for MA organizations beginning in contract year 2028. Specifically, CMS proposes requirements to implement this new law related to provider directory verification and maintenance standards, annual accuracy analysis and reporting, public reporting of accuracy scores, and cost-sharing protections for enrollees who rely on inaccurate provider directory information. CMS also proposes to codify the statutory requirements for contract year 2029, including that CMS must publicly display provider directory accuracy scores in a machine-readable format, and MA organizations must prominently display those scores in their provider directories. Through these proposed codifications implementing the REAL Health Providers Act, CMS seeks to promote provider directory accuracy and accountability among MA organizations to ensure that beneficiaries are provided with clear, accurate information on the network of providers available to meet their healthcare needs. Modernizing and Ensuring Pharmacy Benefit Manager Accountability (Section 6224): CMS has several proposals to implement section 6224 of the CAA, which established requirements related to pharmacy benefit managers (PBMs) and their affiliates specific to the Medicare Part D program. These proposals include new requirements governing the remuneration that PBMs and their affiliates may receive for services in connection with the utilization of Part D drugs, data reporting by PBMs to Part D prescription drug plan (PDP) sponsors and CMS, transparency in PBM price guarantee arrangements, disclosure by PBMs to PDP sponsors of the terms of certain agreements between PBMs or their affiliates and pharmaceutical manufacturers, and other requirements added by the CAA. CMS also proposes to define key terms related to such requirements and establish compliance and enforcement mechanisms, including disgorgement of funds to CMS for violations of the remuneration requirements. The proposed rule will be open for public comment for 60 days following publication in the Federal Register. View this regulation on the Federal Register . The proposed rule will be open for public comment for 60 days following publication in the Federal Register. ### CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification Proposed Rule (CMS 9873-P)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/exchange-pre-eligibility-verification-proposed-rule-cms-9873-p)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `76` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/exchange-pre-eligibility-verification-proposed-rule-cms-9873-p`

Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification Proposed Rule (CMS 9873-P) Introduction Consistent with the President’s goal to prevent waste, fraud, and abuse, today the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services (HHS), through the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS), issued the Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification Notice of Proposed Rulemaking. This rule would align Exchange eligibility and verification regulations with new requirements specified under the Working Families Tax Cuts (WFTC) legislation (P.L. 119-21). The WFTC legislation amended the Internal Revenue Code to establish new eligibility requirements for the premium tax credit (PTC). The Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification proposed rule would establish new Exchange requirements that align advance payments of the premium tax credit (APTC) and cost-sharing reductions (CSRs) eligibility with PTC eligibility changes enacted under sec. 71303 of the WFTC legislation and proposed to be codified in regulations issued by the Department of the Treasury on October 15, 2026. Currently, when consumers apply for coverage through Exchanges, they provide certain information that Exchanges may attempt to verify electronically. If an Exchange cannot verify the information electronically, the consumer enters an inconsistency period during which they need to submit documentation to verify their information. Under existing policy established by the Affordable Care Act (ACA), Exchanges provide APTC and CSRs (collectively, financial assistance) during the inconsistency period to consumers who otherwise appear eligible. If they do not submit documentation to verify their eligibility before the end of the inconsistency period, APTC and CSRs are generally reduced or ended. The Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification proposed rule would require Exchanges to verify the following criteria before providing financial assistance: household income and family size status as a citizen, U.S. national, or eligible noncitizen (including lawfully present) eligibility for or enrollment in other qualifying coverage place of residence However, under the proposed rule, consumers could enroll in Exchange coverage without financial assistance during the inconsistency period while verifications are pending. It would also establish a verification period beginning August 1 before Open Enrollment (OE) each year to ensure that consumers have ample opportunity to provide and confirm information impacting their eligibility before the following coverage year. This proposed rule also includes provisions on annual eligibility redeterminations and re-enrollment, eligibility redeterminations during the plan year, timeline requirements for states seeking to transition to a State-based Exchange, binder payments, a waiver of the proposed verification requirements for certain Special Enrollment Periods (SEPs), effective dates for eligibility determinations, annual OE notices, and plan selection requirements. The proposed regulations would ensure financial assistance is only provided when income can be verified, potentially reducing the risk that consumers would have to repay excess APTC at tax filing due to inaccurate income estimates. They also would reduce improper or fraudulent enrollments by requiring verification of certain eligibility criteria before providing financial assistance, improving program integrity. The provisions in the Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification proposed rule would be effective starting with eligibility determinations made for plan year 2028. The rule would require Exchanges to make a pre-OE verification period available starting August 1 each year ahead of OE, during which consumers could choose to provide eligibility verification information earlier for next coverage year. The first pre-OE window would start August 1, 2027, for 2028 coverage. The Exchange Pre-Eligibility Verification proposed rule is available on the Federal Register at: https://www.federalregister.gov/public-inspection/2026-21891/patient-protection-and-affordable-care-act-exchange-pre-eligibility-verification . Stakeholders may submit public comments for a 30-day period following publication of the proposed rule. Strengthening Program Integrity Requiring Verification Before Receiving Financial Assistance CMS proposes new regulations for verification requirements for consumers requesting financial assistance for Exchange coverage. Exchanges would no longer be permitted to provide financial assistance during inconsistency periods when certain eligibility criteria cannot be verified electronically. Additionally, CMS proposes that Exchanges maintain or reduce the amount of financial assistance provided during such inconsistency periods after an enrollee voluntarily reports a change in circumstances that impacts eligibility for financial help. These new requirements would help ensure that only eligible consumers receive financial assistance. Requiring Annual Verification CMS proposes that currently enrolled consumers would only be automatically re-enrolled with financial assistance if they return to the Exchange to confirm certain eligibility criteria annually and have it re-verified by the Exchange. Consumers who don't actively return and have their information re-verified for financial help would be automatically re-enrolled without financial assistance. The new requirement would help ensure that only eligible consumers receive financial assistance through annual re-enrollment. Supporting Exchange Consumers Opportunity for Verification Before Open Enrollment Beginning August 1, 2027, CMS proposes that Exchanges provide consumers the option to submit certain eligibility information for the Exchange to verify before annual OE. CMS proposes requiring all Exchanges make this pre-OE verification period available to new and returning applicants to provide a minimum set of eligibility information and that all Exchanges start their annual pre-OE verification period on August 1 each year. By providing the early opportunity for verification, the proposed rule would help Exchange enrollees to have their eligibility fully verified before the upcoming coverage year, ensuring continuous coverage with financial assistance from year to year. Flexible Enrollment Options for Consumers CMS proposes requiring Exchanges to allow consumers to select a contingent plan during OE or a SEP such that the plan selection would become effective only if the consumer is determined eligible for financial assistance. This means consumers may choose a plan based on preliminary financial assistance estimates that would only start after they confirm eligibility for financial assistance. This process would enable consumers who elect not to enroll when they do not receive financial assistance during their inconsistency period to enroll once they are determined eligible for financial assistance, even if OE or their SEP, as applicable, has ended. Ensuring Timely Access to Financial Assistance CMS proposes new effective date rules for eligibility determinations and redeterminations made under the other proposed requirements in this rule. These proposals include new effective dates for financial assistance eligibility determinations and redeterminations after successful verification of eligibility. For consumers already enrolled in a health plan when their eligibility for financial assistance is verified, we propose providing financial assistance retroactively for the months during their inconsistency period when they were enrolled. For consumers who select a plan pending verification of eligibility for financial assistance, meaning the plan won’t start until after verification, we propose that their enrollment and financial assistance become effective and will start based on when their eligibility is verified. These proposed rules would provide clarity to consumers, Exchanges, and issuers; ensure timely access to coverage and financial assistance; and limit risks of adverse selection. Flexibility in Open Enrollment Notification Starting with plan year 2028, CMS proposes to permit Exchanges to provide the written annual OE notification to each enrollee before the pre-OE verification period, instead of in the month before OE starts. This would permit Exchanges to design the most effective outreach strategy to educate their consumers about the earlier verification period opportunity and the annual OE. Exception for Changes in Family Size CMS proposes waiving new verification requirements for consumers with certain changes in family size, as outlined in Treasury’s regulations, so that their financial assistance is based on their attested application information during inconsistency periods. This applies when a consumer or their household member qualifies for an SEP triggered by birth, adoption, marriage, or loss of a dependent through divorce, separation, or death (if allowed by the Exchange). Improving Exchange Rules Comment Solicitation on Enhancing Verification Procedures CMS seeks comment on how to strengthen Exchange requirements to verify family size and residency, including which data sources CMS should consider using or making available. CMS seeks comment on ways to increase program integrity while limiting burden on consumers and limiting operational costs and burdens for state and federal Exchanges. State Exchange Transitions CMS proposes that a state submits its application for HHS’ review and approval to become a State-based Exchange 15 months before a state begins operations as a State Exchange for the pre-OE verification period, instead of 15 months before OE. This would ensure the required pre-OE verification period is available to consumers when a State-based Exchange launches, removing the risk that a consumer first verifies on the Federally-facilitated Exchange (FFE) during the pre-OE verification period and then must reverify on the State-based Exchange. This requirement would ensure transitioning states align their transition timeline with the start of the pre-OE verification period, which would be the start of their operations as a State-based Exchange, instead of the start of OE. Binder Payment Alignment CMS proposes that the FFE and State-based Exchanges on the Federal Platform (SBE-FPs) must make prospective and retroactive enrollments under this proposed rule subject to the existing binder payment rules for prospective and retroactive enrollments, respectively. In addition, State-based Exchanges have the option to adopt the same changes. This would give issuers additional flexibility to set binder payment due dates for the proposed new enrollment scenarios. Effective Dates CMS proposes limited updates for current rules on effective dates for financial assistance eligibility redeterminations, when an existing enrollee reports a change to the Exchange during the coverage year. Specifically, in cases where the enrollee does not qualify for an SEP, CMS proposes no longer allowing Exchanges to delay effective dates for such eligibility redeterminations beyond the first of the month after the redetermination occurs. This change would align effective dates across existing and new scenarios and ensure timely eligibility updates. CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Acetic acid chromoendoscopy for detection of neoplastic Barrett's oesophagus: stepped wedge cluster randomised controlled trial (ACID-trial).](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100872)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `75` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42907095`

<h4>Objective</h4>To assess the added value of acetic acid chromoendoscopy (AAC) for the detection of dysplasia and oesophageal adenocarcinoma during Barrett's oesophagus surveillance endoscopies.<h4>Design</h4>Prospective, open label, stepped wedge cluster, randomised trial.<h4>Setting</h4>18 Dutch community hospitals.<h4>Participants</h4>Patients undergoing surveillance for Barrett's oesophagus were eligible, excluding those with known or previously treated dysplasia.<h4>Interventions</h4>Hospitals randomly shifted from surveillance without AAC (control) to AAC (intervention), with endoscopists trained before implementation. Targeted biopsies were obtained if lesions were present; random four quadrant biopsies were conducted.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>The primary endpoint was the neoplasia detection rate, defined as the percentage of participants with low grade dysplasia, high grade dysplasia, and oesophageal adenocarcinoma in any biopsy, confirmed by an experienced gastrointestinal pathologist. The secondary endpoint was the neoplasia detection rate in targeted biopsies.<h4>Results</h4>2267 participants were included, of whom 1438 (63%) were allocated to surveillance without AAC and 829 (37%) to surveillance with AAC; AAC was applied in 679 (82%) allocated participants. Overall, neoplasia was diagnosed in 163 (7.2%) participants (111 (4.9%) low grade dysplasia, 30 (1.3%) high grade dysplasia, and 22 (1.0%) oesophageal adenocarcinoma). AAC use did not increase the neoplasia detection rate (7.4% (50/679) with AAC versus 7.1% (113/1588) without AAC; odds ratio 0.97, 95% confidence interval 0.65 to 1.44; adjusted for calendar time and Barrett's oesophagus length). Suspected lesions were detected in 95 (14.0%) participants receiving AAC versus 101 (6.4%) without AAC (P<0.001), but the targeted neoplasia detection rate was not higher (2.1% (14/679) versus 1.8% (29/1588); odds ratio 1.05, 0.49 to 2.24).<h4>Conclusions</h4>Use of AAC did not improve detection of neoplasia in patients with Barrett's oesophagus undergoing endoscopic surveillance in routine clinical practice.<h4>Trial registration</h4>www.onderzoekmetmensen.nl; NL-OMON25059 (prospective registration; 4 Dec 2019).

### [Intermittent or Continuous Renal-Replacement Therapy in Acute Kidney Injury.](https://doi.org/10.1056/nejmoa2608397)

<h4>Background</h4>The preferred method of renal-replacement therapy for acute kidney injury in critically ill patients remains uncertain.<h4>Methods</h4>In this multicenter, open-label, noninferiority trial, we randomly assigned critically ill adults with acute kidney injury who had indications for renal-replacement therapy to undergo intermittent hemodialysis or continuous renal-replacement therapy as the initial treatment. The primary outcome was a major adverse kidney event - defined as death, dependence on renal-replacement therapy, or a serum creatinine concentration more than 25% above the baseline value - by day 90.<h4>Results</h4>The trial was stopped early after sequential interim analyses showed a sustained signal of higher mortality with intermittent hemodialysis than with continuous renal-replacement therapy. A total of 554 patients underwent randomization, with 272 patients assigned to undergo intermittent hemodialysis and 282 to undergo continuous renal-replacement therapy; 535 patients (260 in the intermittent group and 275 in the continuous group) were included in the intention-to-treat population. A major adverse kidney event occurred by day 90 in 203 patients (78.1%) in the intermittent group and in 197 patients (71.6%) in the continuous group (adjusted absolute risk difference, 6.28 percentage points; 95% confidence interval [CI], -1.22 to 13.79). Death by day 90 occurred in 164 patients (63.1%) and in 147 patients (53.5%), respectively. Kaplan-Meier mortality estimates at day 90 were 63.3% and 53.6% (hazard ratio, 1.30; 95% CI, 1.04 to 1.63). The most common complication potentially related to acute kidney injury or renal-replacement therapy was hypotension, which occurred in 179 patients (68.8%) in the intermittent group and in 183 patients (66.5%) in the continuous group.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Among critically ill patients with acute kidney injury who had indications for renal-replacement therapy, intermittent hemodialysis as the initial treatment approach was not shown to be noninferior to continuous renal-replacement therapy with respect to major adverse kidney events by day 90 and was associated with higher mortality. (Funded by the French Ministry of Health; ICRAKI ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT06032884.).

### [Projected Effect of 2026 Federal Changes to the Childhood Immunization Schedule on Chronic Hepatitis B Virus Infections in the United States.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-26-02135)

<h4>Background</h4>The United States recently proposed changes to the recommendations for childhood hepatitis B virus (HBV) vaccination.<h4>Objective</h4>To project the effect of vaccine policy changes, if fully implemented, on chronic HBV infections.<h4>Design</h4>Markov cohort state transition model of perinatal and childhood HBV infection.<h4>Data sources</h4>Published data on maternal HBV prevalence (0.4%) and hepatitis B surface antigen testing (99.5% sensitivity), childhood vaccine completion (universal birth-dose strategy: 91.1%; deferred strategy: 87.1%; shared decision-making strategy: 50.0%), and age-stratified risk for chronic HBV infection.<h4>Target population</h4>One-year birth cohort of U.S.-born infants (<i>n</i> = 3.6 million).<h4>Time horizon</h4>20 years.<h4>Intervention</h4>Universal birth-dose vaccination, deferred (2-month) vaccination, and vaccination through shared decision making.<h4>Outcome measures</h4>Numbers with chronic HBV infection and susceptibility to HBV, with 95% uncertainty intervals (UIs) derived from probabilistic sensitivity analysis.<h4>Results of base-case analysis</h4>Compared with the universal birth-dose strategy (255 childhood infections [95% UI, 130 to 530]), the deferred and shared decision-making strategies would increase chronic HBV infections in the 2026 birth cohort by 19% to 303 (95% UI, 155 to 610) and by 83% to 467 (95% UI, 209 to 1032), respectively. These strategies would also increase the number of HBV-susceptible people at year 20 (474 000 and 1 806 000) compared with the universal birth-dose strategy (232 000).<h4>Results of sensitivity analysis</h4>In a scenario with lower vaccine coverage (84.5%) and higher HBV prevalence (maternal prevalence of 0.72% and higher risk for horizontal infection), chronic HBV infections would increase to 1700 and 2192, respectively, with the deferred and shared decision-making strategies.<h4>Limitation</h4>Uncertainty in horizontal HBV infection risk.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Proposed policy changes for vaccination through shared decision making are projected to result in an 83% increase in childhood HBV infections, and between 50% and 70% of children entering adulthood annually would be susceptible to HBV infection. Policymakers should reimplement recommendations for universal birth-dose vaccination.<h4>Primary funding source</h4>National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases.

<h4>Importance</h4>International guidelines recommend dobutamine for management of septic shock and cardiac dysfunction with persistent hypoperfusion in adults based on very low-certainty evidence. Randomized trials are lacking on the effect of intraoperative dobutamine combined with norepinephrine vs norepinephrine on postoperative outcomes in patients with septic shock undergoing surgical source control.<h4>Objective</h4>To determine whether intraoperative dobutamine combined with norepinephrine reduces acute kidney injury (AKI) within 7 postoperative days in patients with septic shock undergoing surgical source control.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This randomized, open-label clinical trial in 39 tertiary hospitals in China enrolled adults with septic shock who were undergoing surgical source control between June 2024 and August 2025. Follow-up ended on November 15, 2025.<h4>Intervention</h4>Intraoperative, intravenous infusion of dobutamine (up to 5 µg/kg/min) combined with norepinephrine vs norepinephrine alone.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome was new-onset AKI or worsening of preoperative AKI within 7 days after surgery, defined using Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes (KDIGO) criteria. The 11 secondary outcomes included Sequential Organ Failure Assessment (SOFA) score on postoperative day 1 and urine output 1 hour post intervention.<h4>Results</h4>Among 584 patients (median [IQR] age, 67.5 [55.0-76.0] years; 192 [32.9%] females), new-onset or worsening AKI within 7 postoperative days occurred in 22.8% (66/290) randomized to receive dobutamine plus norepinephrine vs 34.7% (102/294) randomized to receive norepinephrine alone (adjusted risk ratio [ARR], 0.64 [95% CI, 0.49-0.83]). No significant differences were observed in 9 of the 11 secondary outcomes. SOFA scores on postoperative day 1 (adjusted difference, -0.53 [95% CI, -0.98 to -0.07]) were lower and urine output 1 hour post intervention (adjusted difference, 0.55 [95% CI, 0.03 to 1.07] mL/kg/h) was higher in the dobutamine plus norepinephrine group. Stage 2 and 3 AKI were lower in the dobutamine plus norepinephrine group compared with the norepinephrine alone group (stage 2: 4.8% vs 9.9%; ARR, 0.48 [95% CI, 0.26-0.88]; stage 3: 7.9% vs 13.6%; ARR, 0.56 [95% CI, 0.35-0.91]). Tachycardia (heart rate >100/min) occurred more frequently in patients receiving dobutamine and norepinephrine than norepinephrine alone.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among adult patients with septic shock undergoing surgical source control, intraoperative dobutamine combined with norepinephrine reduced the incidence of new-onset or worsening AKI within 7 postoperative days compared with norepinephrine.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT06462313.

### [Human oversight of erroneous clinical AI and decision support a systematic review and meta analysis](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03408-5)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `73` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03408-5`

Artificial intelligence (AI) increasingly supports clinical decisions, but human oversight is protective only if clinicians recognize and resist erroneous output. We systematically reviewed controlled and real-world studies of clinician responses to incorrect automated advice. Forty-six reports represented 41 independent cohort clusters, including 40 core cohorts. Across three adjusted cohorts, clinician correctness was substantially higher with correct than incorrect automated advice (pooled OR 10.19, 95% CI 7.43–13.98; I² = 0%); a ≥ 2020 sensitivity analysis was similar but imprecise (OR 9.22, 95% CI 2.06–41.13). In four heterogeneous real-world computerized ECG studies, 26.6% of erroneous interpretations were not corrected or recognized (95% CI 6.4–65.6%; I² = 95.8%). Contemporary AI and large language model studies also documented harmful reliance but were too heterogeneous for pooling. Patient-level adverse events were reported in one retrospective cohort. Human oversight should be evaluated as an error-interception capability rather than assumed to ensure safety.

### [Continuous Glucose Monitoring in Non-Insulin-Treated Type 2 Diabetes.](https://doi.org/10.1056/nejmoa2608571)

<h4>Background</h4>Continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) has been shown to be beneficial in persons with type 1 diabetes and others with insulin-treated type 2 diabetes, but its role in the treatment of persons with type 2 diabetes not being treated with insulin has needed further study.<h4>Methods</h4>In a 26-week trial conducted at 22 primary care practices in the United States, we randomly assigned adults with type 2 diabetes who were not using insulin to either CGM or routine care (control). The primary outcome was the glycated hemoglobin level at 26 weeks. Key CGM-measured secondary outcomes were tested hierarchically to control the type I error rate.<h4>Results</h4>A total of 145 participants were assigned to the CGM group, and 138 to the routine-care group. Participants ranged in age from 21 to 84 years. The mean (±SD) glycated hemoglobin level decreased from 8.8±1.3% at baseline to 7.2±1.1% at 26 weeks in the CGM group and from 8.7±1.2% to 8.0±1.4% in the routine-care group (adjusted mean difference, -0.9 percentage points; 95% confidence interval [CI], -1.1 to -0.6; P<0.001). The mean percentage of time that the CGM-measured glucose level was in the glucose-level range of 70 to 180 mg per deciliter was 21 percentage points higher (95% CI, 16 to 26) with CGM than with routine care (P<0.001). The between-group difference in the percentage of time that the glucose level was below 54 mg per deciliter was -0.17 percentage points (95% CI, -0.27 to -0.11; P<0.001 for noninferiority); the difference in the percentage of time that the glucose level was below 70 mg per deciliter was -0.20 percentage points (95% CI, -0.43 to -0.09; P<0.001 for noninferiority; P = 0.004 for superiority). No severe hypoglycemia events were reported.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Among adults with type 2 diabetes that was not being treated with insulin and was being managed in a primary care setting, the use of CGM led to a greater reduction in the glycated hemoglobin level and to a lower risk of hyperglycemia than routine care. (Funded by Dexcom; CONNECT ClinicalTrials.gov number, NCT06601166.).

### [Diagnosis and Treatment of Cardiovascular Conditions in Medicare Advantage In-Home Health Risk Assessments.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-26-01366)

- Source: `annals_internal_abstracts` · published `2026-10-20` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `72` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42896184`

<h4>Background</h4>Medicare Advantage insurers conduct in-home health risk assessments (HRAs) to identify high-risk patients and facilitate appropriate care. Critics argue this practice increases risk-adjusted payments without improving quality. Comprehensive data on use of in-home HRAs and downstream treatment are lacking.<h4>Objective</h4>To examine trends in cardiovascular conditions diagnosed during in-home HRAs, characteristics associated with HRA-based diagnoses, and treatment rates after diagnoses.<h4>Design</h4>Cohort study.<h4>Setting</h4>Population-based.<h4>Participants</h4>Medicare Advantage beneficiaries.<h4>Measurements</h4>Using 100% Medicare claims, trends in the proportion of cardiovascular conditions diagnosed during in-home HRAs (vs. facility-based visits) were examined. Multivariable logistic regression models were fit to estimate the association between beneficiary- and plan-level characteristics and HRA-based diagnoses and compare treatment rates across settings.<h4>Results</h4>Among 21 496 506 Medicare Advantage beneficiaries diagnosed with a cardiovascular risk factor or disease, the proportion diagnosed during in-home HRAs increased between 2018 and 2022 for hypertension (45.9% to 47.7%), hyperlipidemia (57.8% to 66.7%), diabetes (47.8% to 51.6%), atrial fibrillation (30.0% to 36.0%), ischemic heart disease (37.2% to 41.6%), peripheral vascular disease (64.5% to 73.9%), and heart failure (34.7% to 42.6%). Racial and ethnic minority beneficiaries and those enrolled in lower-rated plans were more likely to receive HRA-based diagnoses. Treatment rates were substantially lower after HRA-based diagnoses across all conditions.<h4>Limitations</h4>Differences in the severity of conditions between groups could not be assessed. Prescription fills were used as a proxy for management. Observed differences in the diagnosis and treatment of conditions across encounter settings may be susceptible to unmeasured confounding.<h4>Conclusion</h4>Among Medicare Advantage beneficiaries, an increasingly large share of cardiovascular conditions are being diagnosed through in-home HRAs, but treatment rates after these diagnoses are consistently lower than after facility-based encounters.<h4>Primary funding source</h4>National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute.

### [Lymph node-targeted, mKRAS-specific amphiphile vaccine in pancreatic cancer: the randomized phase 2 AMPLIFY-7P trial](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04734-7)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `72` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04734-7`

Camupepimut (ELI-002 7P) drives mutated KRAS (mKRAS)-specific T cell responses that correlated with disease-free survival (DFS) and overall survival (OS) in patients with mKRAS pancreatic adenocarcinoma (PDAC) in the phase 1 AMPLIFY-201 trial. In the phase 2 AMPLIFY-7P trial, patients with stage I-III KRAS-mutated PDAC without recurrence within 6 months of standard therapy were randomized 2:1 to camupepimut (n = 96) or standard-of-care/observation (n = 48). Stratification balanced nodal status. The primary DFS endpoint was not met (median 23.8 versus 21.2 months; HR 0.85, P = 0.5329). However, prespecified and exploratory analyses were informative. A numerical DFS advantage at 6 months of 14.0% (75.7% versus 61.7%; HR 0.55, P = 0.0557) corresponded to camupepimut dosing. Post hoc multivariate Cox regression analysis of DFS indicated an improved outcome for camupepimut (adjusted HR 0.47, P = 0.0086), a worse outcome for stage III disease (adjusted HR 2.29, P = 0.0127), an elevated baseline CA19-9 (adjusted HR 3.16, P &lt; 0.0001) and baseline circulating tumor DNA (ctDNA) positivity (adjusted HR 5.99, P &lt; 0.0001). Prespecified subgroup analyses addressing prognostic and predictive factors suggested DFS benefit for camupepimut (ctDNA negative: HR 0.51, P = 0.0280; no prior radiation: HR 0.59, P = 0.0221). Exploratory analysis demonstrated that ex vivo mKRAS-specific T cell responses were robust (98.9%, 89/90) and associated with antigen spreading (86.7%, 13/15). In camupepimut-treated patients, mKRAS-specific T cell response &gt;9.17-fold over baseline (82.2%, 74/90) correlated with improved DFS (HR 0.22, P &lt; 0.0001), with the median DFS not reached versus 6.08 months for above and below 9.17-fold, respectively. Camupepimut safety was similar to observation, with grade ≥3 adverse events in 10.4% (10/96) versus 14.6% (7/48), and few serious AEs (6.3%, 6/96 versus 8.3%, 4/48; reflecting progression/surgical complication). These efficacy and immunogenicity signals will inform a refined strategy for a confirmatory phase 3 trial. ClinicalTrials.gov identifier: NCT05726864 . As presented at the ESMO Congress 2026, in a randomized phase 2 trial evaluating an off-the-shelf lymph node-targeting peptide vaccine in patients with mutated KRAS pancreatic cancer within 6 months of standard curative intent therapy, the primary endpoint of disease-free survival was not met, but prespecified subgroup analyses showed significant disease-free survival benefit in the vaccine arm for patients with circulating tumor DNA-negative disease and in those without prior radiation treatment.

### [Fluid Therapy in Mechanically Ventilated Critically Ill Adult Patients.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.21291)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-14` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `71` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42872606`

<h4>Importance</h4>Fluid therapy is ubiquitous in the intensive care unit (ICU) but carries risks, especially in mechanically ventilated patients. Contemporary global practice across dose, indication, and type is unknown.<h4>Objective</h4>To characterize ICU fluid practices in mechanically ventilated adults.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>International prospective observational cohort study between April 2024 and July 2025, across 206 ICUs in 41 countries on 6 continents. Consecutive adult patients receiving invasive mechanical ventilation within 3 days of ICU admission and ventilated 24 hours or more. Exclusion criteria were age younger than 16 years and transfer under mechanical ventilation from another ICU.<h4>Exposures</h4>Fluid volume, indication, and type after intubation.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Key measures included (1) total infused volume and daily fluid balance over 7 days after intubation; (2) the indication for fluid therapy over the first 3 days after intubation; (3) the proportion of total administered fluid volume attributable to each fluid type over the first 3 days after intubation.<h4>Results</h4>Among 2454 patients analyzed (median age, 63 years [IQR, 49-73 years]; 63.6% [1560] male), median daily fluid input peaked on day 1 at 3.0 L (IQR, 2.2-4.0 L) and declined to 2.6 L (IQR, 1.8-3.5 L) through day 6. Daily fluid balance (median, 0.9 L; IQR, 0.1-1.9 L on day 1) decreased to less than 0.3 L (IQR, -0.5 to 1.2 L) by day 3 and remained near-neutral through day 6. Maintenance and replacement fluids (to meet daily requirements and to correct ongoing losses; 31.9%) and creep fluids (to deliver medications and maintain catheter patency; 30.9%) were the primary indications, whereas resuscitation (to restore circulating blood volume; 16.0%) accounted for a smaller proportion. Isotonic balanced crystalloids (48.9%) and isotonic saline (0.9% sodium chloride) (27.0%) were common; colloids were rare (1.3%). Substantial between-site variation persisted after adjustment for case-mix.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In this prospective international cohort study, fluid therapy varied widely among mechanically ventilated critically ill patients, with maintenance and creep fluids-rather than resuscitation fluids-accounting for most total volume.

The primary outcome occurred in 243 of 549 patients (44.3%) in the individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy group vs 256 of 546 patients (46.9%) in the routine hemodynamic therapy group (relative risk, 0.94 [95.1% CI, 0.83-1.08]; P = .38). There were no between-group differences for the incidence of the individual components of the primary outcome within the first 7 postoperative days.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Among high-risk patients undergoing elective major abdominal surgery, individualized cardiac index-guided hemodynamic therapy targeting the patient's preoperative resting cardiac index did not reduce the incidence of a composite outcome of acute kidney injury, acute myocardial injury, severe infectious complications, nonfatal cardiac arrest, or death within the first 7 postoperative days compared with routine hemodynamic therapy.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT05648279.

### [Childhood Cancer Risk After Assisted Reproductive Technology.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18862)

<h4>Importance</h4>Childhood cancer risk after assisted reproductive technology (ART) remains uncertain, particularly by procedure and cancer type.<h4>Objective</h4>To examine associations between ART and childhood cancer overall and by ART modality and cancer type.<h4>Design, setting, and population</h4>Nationwide Danish cohort of 1 086 136 live-born children from 1996 to 2012, of whom 37 228 (3.4%) were conceived using ART, with follow-up through 2023.<h4>Exposures</h4>Maternal ART, classified as in vitro fertilization (IVF), intracytoplasmic sperm injection, or frozen embryo transfer.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Incident childhood cancer. Cox proportional hazards models estimated hazard ratios (HRs); Poisson models estimated incidence rates. Non-ART births were the primary reference; secondary analyses used children of women with a history of infertility and children conceived using IVF as reference groups.<h4>Results</h4>During a median follow-up of 20 years, 3736 cancers occurred. Compared with non-ART births (incidence rate, 18.3 per 100 000 person-years), children conceived using ART had higher cancer incidence (incidence rate, 23.6 per 100 000; HR, 1.26 [95% CI, 1.07-1.48]). Rates were higher after frozen embryo transfer (incidence rate, 41.1 per 100 000; HR, 2.18 [95% CI, 1.45-3.29]) and intracytoplasmic sperm injection (incidence rate, 27.9 per 100 000; HR, 1.48 [95% CI, 1.16-1.89]) but not IVF (incidence rate, 18.5 per 100 000; HR, 1.01 [95% CI, 0.79-1.28]). Frozen embryo transfer was associated with leukemia (incidence rate, 11.1 per 100 000; HR, 2.36 [95% CI, 1.06-5.28]), lymphomas (HR, 3.03 [95% CI, 1.13-8.12]), and soft tissue sarcomas (HR, 7.63 [95% CI, 2.82-20.62]); intracytoplasmic sperm injection was associated with lymphomas (incidence rate, 4.6 per 100 000; HR, 1.94 [95% CI, 1.07-3.54]), soft tissue sarcomas (incidence rate, 3.3 per 100 000; HR, 3.28 [95% CI, 1.54-7.00]), and central nervous system neoplasms (incidence rate, 8.2 per 100 000; HR, 1.63 [95% CI, 1.04-2.57]); no associations were observed for IVF. Findings were similar in secondary analyses using children of women with a history of infertility and children conceived using IVF as reference groups.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>ART, particularly frozen embryo transfer and intracytoplasmic sperm injection, was associated with increased childhood cancer risk, but absolute risks were low. The consistent results in secondary analyses of children with maternal infertility history or conceived via IVF suggest that procedure-related, rather than infertility-related, factors warrant further investigation.

<h4>Importance</h4>The optimal vasopressor dose at which to initiate a second vasopressor in patients with septic shock is uncertain.<h4>Objective</h4>To compare the effects of hospital-level strategies initiating vasopressin infusion at a lower threshold vs a higher threshold on outcomes in patients with septic shock.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Cluster-randomized, cluster-crossover, pragmatic clinical strategy trial including adult patients receiving vasopressors for septic shock at 13 hospitals in Idaho and Utah from February 1, 2024, to July 31, 2025. Hospitals were randomized for the first month to an initial strategy (6 hospitals to the lower threshold vs 7 hospitals to the higher threshold) and then crossed over to the alternate strategy monthly thereafter.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The primary outcome of 28-day mortality and the key secondary outcome of kidney replacement therapy-free days through day 28 were analyzed using multivariable generalized linear mixed-effects models, accounting for trial site and baseline patient characteristics.<h4>Results</h4>There were 3959 unique patients with septic shock (median age, 68 [IQR, 57 to 77] years; 1903 [48%] were female). Of the patients more likely to experience differential management under the tested hospital-level vasopressin dose strategies (maximum vasopressor dose ≥0.07 µg/kg/min of norepinephrine or equivalent), 1034 of 1521 (68.0%) cared for under the lower-threshold strategy received vasopressin compared with 651 of 1526 (42.7%) cared for under the higher-threshold strategy. In the hospital-level strategy recommending a lower-dose threshold for vasopressin initiation, the maximum vasopressor dose received prior to vasopressin initiation was a median of 0.14 (IQR, 0.10 to 0.22) µg/kg/min of norepinephrine or equivalent vs 0.34 (IQR, 0.16 to 0.42) µg/kg/min of norepinephrine or equivalent in the hospital-level strategy recommending a higher-dose threshold for vasopressin initiation. Mortality at 28 days occurred in 628 of 1975 patients (31.8%) treated with the hospital-level strategy recommending initiation of vasopressin at a lower-dose threshold of other vasopressors vs 655 of 1984 patients (33.0%) treated with the higher-threshold strategy (adjusted marginal odds ratio, 0.98 [95% CI, 0.87 to 1.06], P = .62; adjusted risk difference, -0.4 [95% CI, -2.7 to 1.3] percentage points). Patients in both strategies for vasopressin initiation had a median of 29 (IQR, -1 to 29) kidney replacement therapy-free days (adjusted marginal odds ratio, 1.01 [95% CI, 0.91 to 1.14]).<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In this large, pragmatic clinical trial comparing the effectiveness of 2 hospital-level strategies for second-line vasopressor management in septic shock, a strategy recommending initiation of vasopressin at a lower dose of other vasopressors did not reduce 28-day mortality compared with a strategy recommending vasopressin initiation at a higher dose of other vasopressors.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT06217562.

### [Medicare Part D 2027 National Average Monthly Bid Amount Information](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-part-d-2027-national-average-monthly-bid-amount-information)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-07-28` · freshness `stale`
- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/medicare-part-d-2027-national-average-monthly-bid-amount-information`

Medicare Part D 2027 National Average Monthly Bid Amount Information On July 28, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) released preliminary technical Medicare Part D bid information for contract year (CY) 2027 to help Part D plan sponsors finalize their Part D and Medicare Advantage (MA) offerings and prepare for Medicare Open Enrollment. Additionally, CMS is announcing the conclusion of the Part D Premium Stabilization Demonstration, a voluntary demonstration for standalone prescription drug plans implemented in CY 2025 to address volatility and variation in standalone premiums following benefit changes mandated by the Inflation Reduction Act (IRA). For CY 2027, CMS bid analysis indicates that Part D plan sponsors had sufficient experience under the redesigned Part D benefit to support their assumptions in developing the prescription drug plan bids. Therefore, CMS will discontinue the demonstration at the end of CY 2026 to return the program to operating under traditional market conditions in CY 2027. As in past years, CMS will release the 2027 MA and Part D landscape in mid-to-late September, once all offerings are finalized. In addition, CMS will release final average MA and Part D premiums, along with other key information, in September with the MA and Part D landscape. National Average Monthly Bid Amount and Government Subsidy The national average monthly bid amount (NAMBA) is an enrollment-weighted average of all applicable Part D plan bids for basic Part D benefits, with weights based on the number of enrollees in each plan. The NAMBA is used to calculate the government subsidy for plans. In 2027, the NAMBA will be $296.05. Base Beneficiary Premium for 2027 The national base beneficiary premium is the starting point for calculating a plan-specific basic Part D premium. This value is calculated per a statutory formula, using a percentage of bids and estimates of reinsurance costs submitted by certain Part D plans for the required statutory minimum level of coverage (known as the “basic” benefit). Between 2024 and 2029, the annual increase in the base beneficiary premium is capped by the IRA’s premium stabilization provision not to exceed 6% per year. For 2027, the base beneficiary premium will be $41.33. ### CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

- Reading value: `pending` · status: `ineligible`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/fiscal-year-2027-medicare-inpatient-psychiatric-facility-prospective-payment-system-final-rule-cms`

Fiscal Year 2027 Medicare Inpatient Psychiatric Facility Prospective Payment System Final Rule (CMS-1847-F) On July 29, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a final rule to update Medicare payment policies and rates for Inpatient Psychiatric Facilities (IPFs) under the IPF Prospective Payment System (PPS) (CMS-1847-F) for fiscal year (FY) 2027. CMS is publishing this final rule consistent with its statutory authority to update Medicare payment policies for IPFs annually. This fact sheet discusses the provisions of the final rule, including annual updates to the prospective payment rates, the outlier threshold, the wage index, and associated impact analysis. In addition, the rule finalizes a policy effective for FY 2028 (October 1, 2027) to limit outlier payments at the facility level to no more than 20% of an IPF’s total IPF PPS payments in a year, for IPFs with at least 50 stays per year. For the IPF Quality Reporting Program, CMS is removing two measures from the program, as well as implementing a standardized IPF patient assessment instrument. Changes to Payments Under the IPF PPS Updates to IPF Payment Rates For FY 2027, CMS is updating the IPF PPS payment rates by 2.3%, based on the 2021-based IPF market basket increase of 3.2% less a 0.9 percentage point productivity adjustment. Additionally, CMS is updating the outlier threshold so that estimated outlier payments remain at 2% of total IPF PPS payments. Total estimated payments to IPFs would increase by 2.3%, or $60 million, in FY 2027, relative to IPF payments in FY 2026. Reform of IPF PPS Outlier Payment Policy The IPF PPS includes an outlier adjustment to promote access to IPF care for those patients who require expensive care and to limit the financial risk of IPFs treating unusually costly patients. Providing additional payments to IPFs for extremely costly cases strongly improves the accuracy of the IPF PPS in determining resource costs at the patient and facility level. Outlier payments are determined by comparing the estimated cost of an IPF stay to a threshold value. The outlier fixed dollar loss threshold is set each year so that estimated outlier payments equal 2% of total IPF PPS payments. In recent years, the outlier threshold has increased significantly, and commenters have expressed concerns that it is increasingly difficult to receive outlier payments for costly IPF stays. Our analysis of IPF PPS outlier payments has shown that certain IPFs have exceptionally high reported costs and receive outlier payments on many of their claims. We note that these providers’ high overall costs are primarily driven by high routine costs (for example, labor, real estate, or overhead), which are fixed at the provider level and do not vary from one patient to another. We are finalizing the proposal to cap outlier payments at the provider level to minimize the impact of these high-cost facilities on the outlier pool. However, we are deferring the effective date of this policy until FY 2028 to provide additional time to evaluate the cost drivers identified by commenters. In addition, we are finalizing an exception to this cap policy for facilities with fewer than 50 stays per year. Updates to the IPF Quality Reporting Program The IPF Quality Reporting Program requires that all IPFs paid under the IPF PPS submit certain specified quality data to CMS, in a form and manner and within the timeframes that CMS prescribes. IPFs that do not submit the specified data on quality measures as required by the IPF Quality Reporting Program receive a 2-percentage-point reduction to their annual payment update. The IPF Quality Reporting Program aims to assess and improve the quality of care provided to IPF patients. By requiring IPFs to submit quality data to CMS and by CMS publicly reporting such data under the IPF Quality Reporting Program, CMS ensures that patients can make more informed decisions about their healthcare options. In this final rule, CMS is making changes to quality measures in the IPF Quality Reporting Program. CMS is removing two measures beginning with the CY 2026 reporting period/FY 2028 payment determination: the Alcohol Use Brief Intervention Provided or Offered (SUB-2) and subset Alcohol Use Brief Intervention (SUB-2a) measure and the Tobacco Use Treatment Provided or Offered at Discharge (TOB-3) and subset Tobacco Use Treatment at Discharge (TOB-3a) measure. CMS is also implementing a standardized IPF patient assessment instrument (IPF-PAI), as mandated by section 4125(b)(1) of the Consolidated Appropriations Act of 2023 (CAA, 2023). IPFs will have two methods to submit IPF-PAI data to CMS. IPFs can use a free CMS-developed web application called the Patient Assessment Reporting Interoperability Tool (PARIT) or can submit IPF-PAI data using Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) CMS has built from the Health Level Seven (HL7®) Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources (FHIR®) specification. This will be the first CMS statutory quality reporting program to use the FHIR standard to support patient assessment data submission. The final rule can be viewed at the Federal Register at: https://www.federalregister.gov/public-inspection/current . Information on the Inpatient Psychiatric Facility PPS is available at: https://www.cms.gov/medicare/payment/prospective-payment-systems/inpatient-psychiatric-facility . Information on the Inpatient Psychiatric Facility Quality Reporting Program is available at: https://www.cms.gov/medicare/quality/initiatives/hospital-quality-initiative/inpatient-psychiatric-facility-quality-reporting-ipfqr-program . ### CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145

### [Fiscal Year 2027 Skilled Nursing Facility Prospective Payment System Final Rule (CMS 1843-F)](https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/fiscal-year-2027-skilled-nursing-facility-prospective-payment-system-final-rule-cms-1843-f)

- Source: `cms_fact_sheets` · published `2026-07-29` · freshness `stale`

- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.cms.gov/newsroom/fact-sheets/fiscal-year-2027-skilled-nursing-facility-prospective-payment-system-final-rule-cms-1843-f`

Fiscal Year 2027 Skilled Nursing Facility Prospective Payment System Final Rule (CMS 1843-F) On July 29, 2026, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) issued a final rule for updates to Medicare payment policies and rates for skilled nursing facilities under the Skilled Nursing Facility (SNF) Prospective Payment System (PPS) for fiscal year (FY) 2027. CMS is publishing this final rule consistent with the legal requirements to update Medicare payment policies for SNFs annually. This fact sheet discusses the final rule’s provisions. FY 2027 Final Updates to the SNF Payment Rates For FY 2027, CMS finalized updating SNF PPS rates by 2.4% based on the final SNF market basket of 3.3%, reduced by a 0.9% productivity adjustment, for an estimated increase of $882.74 million in aggregate payments to SNFs. FY 2027 Final Updates to the SNF Quality Reporting Program Measure Removal. CMS is finalizing the removal of two measures from the SNF Quality Reporting Program ( QRP), beginning with the FY 2028 SNF QRP: (1) the COVID-19 Vaccination Coverage Among Healthcare Personnel measure, and (2) the COVID-19 Vaccine: Percent of Patients/Residents Who Are Up to Date measure. Data Submission Deadline. CMS is finalizing the revised data submission timeframe from 4.5 months to approximately 45 days, beginning with the FY 2029 SNF QRP. This shortened timeframe will reduce the lag between data submission and public reporting by up to three months, resulting in timelier data for consumers and their families. SNFs will also have earlier access to data to support their quality initiatives. Minimum Data Set ( MDS) Data Submission. To obtain the most accurate SNF quality of care information and to remain relevant to the SNF community and consumers, CMS is finalizing a requirement for all SNFs to submit MDS data for all SNF residents receiving covered skilled care, regardless of payer. This will align the SNF QRP with other Post-Acute Care settings and CMS programs that already collect data on all patients regardless of payer. FY 2027 SNF QRP Request for Information CMS received comments on one potential measure topic that might be adopted in future years for the SNF QRP: advanced care planning (ACP). ACP is a continuous process of conversation and documentation to align a patient’s care and interventions with their beliefs, values, and preferences if they become unable to make those decisions. CMS provides a summary of comments received in the FY 2027 SNF PPS final rule. FY 2027 Final Updates to the SNF Value-Based Purchasing Program For the SNF Value-Based Purchasing (VBP) Program, CMS finalized performance standards for the FY 2029 and FY 2030 program years to comply with the program’s statutory notice deadline. CMS will update the “snapshot date” codified at 42 CFR § 413.338(f)(1)(v) for two measures calculated using MDS assessment data to maintain alignment with the newly finalized SNF QRP submission deadlines for MDS assessment data, beginning with FY 2027 data. The SNF VBP adjustments for certain SNFs subject to the net reduction in payments under the SNF VBP and which are not incorporated into the impact estimates for the payment rate are an estimated $203.60 million reduction in FY 2027. Request for Information on Updating the Patient Driven Payment Model Payment System to Address Case-Mix Upcoding This Request for Information (RFI) focused on potential updates to the Patient Driven Payment Model payment system as part of a broader effort to ensure that payment policy reflects current care practices and changes in the SNF resident population. The RFI also focused on how CMS could address observed case-mix upcoding. CMS will take the comments received under advisement in any potential proposed adjustments in future rulemaking. The SNF Prospective Payment System Final Rule can be viewed on the Federal Register at: https://www.federalregister.gov/public-inspection/current CMS News and Media Group Catherine Howden, Director Media Inquiries Form 202-690-6145
