### [Bat-borne zoonoses: transmission, impact, prevention, and management.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100560)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-14` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `55` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42875048`

Climate change and biodiversity loss are global environmental crises driving emerging infectious diseases, including bat-borne pathogens of public health significance: Ebola, Marburg, Nipah, Hendra, classic rabies virus, and Australian bat lyssavirus, as well as the fungus <i>Histoplasma</i> This review presents the evidence on the mechanisms of transmission, impact, and prevention and management strategies relevant to each of these pathogens. Transmission pathways vary between pathogens but often involve exposure to body fluids or excreta of a bat or other infected animal or human. The diseases associated with these infections have high mortality rates, and survivors may continue to experience a range of long term effects, including neurological and neuropsychiatric signs and symptoms. Prevention strategies may be implemented to reduce the risk of zoonotic spillover and human-to-human transmission of these pathogens, including better ecological stewardship, reducing exposure of domestic animals and humans to bats, isolation of infected people, and, where possible, vaccination of humans and animals. The mainstay of treatment for these conditions is supportive care; however, specific drugs are now available for histoplasmosis and <i>Ebolavirus</i> infection. In the face of climate change and further loss of biodiversity, developing more effective strategies to prevent and manage these conditions will be critical.

### [Evaluation of the accuracy, completeness, and compliance of current summary reporting of adverse events to the Food and Drug Administration using the manufacturer and user facility device experience (MAUDE) database: cross sectional study.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100812)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-14` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `64` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42875046`

<h4>Objectives</h4>To evaluate the accuracy and completeness of adverse event summary reports for medical devices and compliance of the reports with the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) requirements.<h4>Design</h4>Cross sectional study.<h4>Setting</h4>The FDA manufacturer and user facility device experience (MAUDE) database.<h4>Participants</h4>All adverse event summary reports filed in MAUDE from July 2019 to June 2025.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>Identification of device problem codes, appropriate identification as summary reports in the submission form, concordance between the number of events data field and the report narrative, and compliance with FDA reporting deadlines.<h4>Results</h4>6308 summary reports were submitted to MAUDE containing 159 503 adverse events. Use of summary reports increased from 808 to 1757 between 2020 and 2024, a factor of 1.21 per year (P<0.001). Most (91%) adverse events in summary reports were for class 3 high risk devices. Summary reports lacked descriptions of potential device related problems: 57% of reports denoted an "adverse event without identified device or use problem" and 38% of reports selected "insufficient information" in the device problem code data field. Manufacturers incorrectly checked "N" (no) in the summary report (Y/N, reporting yes/no) field for 694 summary reports (11%) and erroneously listed "n=1" under the number of events field for 423 summary reports (7%), causing an undercount of 11 902 adverse events. The majority (55%) of summary reports failed to comply with the FDA reporting deadline of 30 days after the manufacturer became aware of the adverse event.<h4>Conclusions</h4>Adverse event summary reports in MAUDE are frequently incomplete, inaccurate, and delayed. Improvements are needed to ensure timely identification of device safety signals and uphold FDA transparency standards.

### [Isoflurane-Based Inhaled Sedation in Mechanically Ventilated Adults With Acute Hypoxemic Respiratory Failure: The SAVE-ICU Randomized Clinical Trial.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18471)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-14` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `68` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42872958`

<h4>Importance</h4>Rigorous evidence for inhaled sedation using isoflurane in mechanically ventilated patients with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure (AHRF) is limited.<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether an inhaled sedation strategy lowers hospital mortality compared with usual care intravenous sedation strategy in mechanically ventilated adults with AHRF.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>Pragmatic, effectiveness, open-label, superiority, randomized clinical trial in 14 intensive care units (ICUs) in Canada. Adults (≥18 years) receiving sedation for less than 72 hours and receiving mechanical ventilation for AHRF from non-COVID respiratory failure (ratio of partial pressure of arterial oxygen [Pao2] to fraction of inspired oxygen [Fio2] <300 mm Hg) or COVID-19 were enrolled between September 2020 and March 2025 with follow-up through June 2025.<h4>Intervention</h4>Patients were randomized to an inhaled sedation strategy using isoflurane with intravenous adjuncts as clinically needed or to a usual intravenous sedation care strategy. Sedatives were titrated to achieve the desired target sedation score guided by clinical teams and continued until extubation or death.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>Primary outcome was all-cause hospital mortality. Secondary outcomes included ICU- and ventilator-free days at day 30; hospital-free days at day 60; use of refractory hypoxemia adjunctive therapies; delirium- and coma-free days at day 14; and quality-of-life and disability scores at 3 months.<h4>Results</h4>Of 786 patients randomized, 780 were included in the primary analysis (mean age, 61.1 years; 34.0% female; 73.7% non-COVID AHRF, mean Pao2:Fio2 ratio, 152.6 mm Hg); 385 in the inhaled sedation and 395 in the intravenous sedation groups. In the inhaled sedation group, 49.9% to 55.4% of patients received concomitant intravenous sedation on any day during the first 14 days after randomization. At hospital discharge, 161 of 379 patients (42.5%) in the inhaled sedation group had died, compared with 171 of 390 patients (43.8%) in the intravenous sedation group (risk ratio, 0.98 [95% CI, 0.84 to 1.14]; P = .85). There was no effect modification in prespecified subgroup analyses for age, sex, and COVID-19 status. ICU-, ventilator-, and hospital-free days were similar between groups. The inhaled sedation group received more adjunctive therapies for hypoxemia (239 [63.9%] vs 214 [55.0%]; between-group difference, 8.89% [95% CI, 1.95% to 15.83%]) and had fewer delirium- and coma-free days (between-group difference, -0.95 [95% CI, -1.69 to -0.22]). There was no difference in quality-of-life and disability scores between groups.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>In mechanically ventilated adults with AHRF, an inhaled sedation strategy using isoflurane with intravenous adjuncts as clinically needed did not reduce hospital mortality compared with an intravenous sedation strategy.<h4>Trial registration</h4>ClinicalTrials.gov Identifier: NCT04415060.

### [Clinician Use of an Artificial Intelligence-Enabled Clinical Information Platform.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.21868)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `69` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42879271`

<h4>Importance</h4>Despite growing interest in the use of artificial intelligence (AI) in medicine, empirical evidence on how clinicians use these tools is limited.<h4>Objective</h4>To characterize clinician use of an AI-enabled clinical information platform, the distribution of use across the clinical workforce, and the purpose of submitted queries.<h4>Design, setting, and participants</h4>This cross-sectional study analyzed clinicians with National Provider Identifier-verified accounts of a commonly used AI-enabled clinical information platform, OpenEvidence, from January 1, 2024, through June 30, 2026. A large language model was used to classify the clinical purpose and topic areas of a stratified random sample of 38.8 million queries.<h4>Main outcomes and measures</h4>The number of active users and clinical queries, the share of queries relative to representation in the national workforce, and the clinical purpose and topic of queries were examined.<h4>Results</h4>Overall, 952 008 clinicians (58.5% female), including 556 200 physicians, submitted 271 million queries during the 2.5-year study period. Monthly active users increased from 355 in January 2024 to 703 265 in June 2026, and average monthly queries per active user rose from 18.0 to 39.5. Nurse practitioners (44.5) and physician assistants (44.5) submitted more queries per active user-month than physicians (37.1). A higher share of queries relative to the national workforce was submitted by physicians within a decade of graduation (use ratio, 2.54) and residents and fellows (1.84). Use was also higher than average among physicians specializing in oncology and hematology (use ratio, 2.00), internal medicine (1.88), and family medicine (1.80), but lower in procedural and diagnostic specialties (ophthalmology, 0.23; radiology, 0.24; orthopedics, 0.24). Most queries addressed clinical decision-making (51.9%), particularly treatment and management (33.1%), whereas diagnosis accounted for 13.9% of queries. Queries addressing topics outside physicians' own specialties ranged from 35% among oncologists and hematologists to 88% among emergency department physicians.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Over 2.5 years, more than 950 000 US clinicians submitted greater than 270 million queries to an AI-enabled clinical information platform. Use varied across clinician types, specialties, and career stages and was frequently for treatment decisions rather than diagnostic tasks.

### [Same Name, Moving Target: Model Version Drift in Syntheses of Large Language Models for Mental Health](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e111307/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-15` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e111307/`

This letter comments on the systematic review and meta-analysis of large language models (LLMs) in mental health care by Leung and colleagues (JMIR AI 2026;5:e87730). The bivariate meta-analysis with Hartung-Knapp adjustments is methodologically sound, and its cautious conclusions are warranted. I raise an unaddressed threat to the pooled estimates: model version drift. Over the search window (November 2022 to March 2026), &quot;GPT-4&quot; did not denote a single intervention; released in March 2023, it was followed by API snapshots whose behavior differed materially, with accuracy on a prime-number identification task falling from 84% to 51% between the March and June 2023 versions. Because the review extracted only model name and model type and did not capture version or testing dates, the estimates pooled under GPT-4 (k=4) and GPT-3.5 (k=4) may each span several substantively different models, and the pooled GPT-4 estimate may correspond to no single system a clinician could deploy. This concern is distinct from the acknowledged limitation that few studies evaluated the newest variants, which concerns currency rather than comparability. I suggest reporting model version and testing date for each pooled study (consistent with TRIPOD-LLM item 6a), examining robustness to version mixing in sensitivity analyses, and qualifying the abstract comparative statements accordingly. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/e770a7e6754928129106feabee847a04" />

### [AI for Poststroke Epilepsy: Scoping Review of Imaging-Based Prediction Models and Implications for African Settings](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e96828/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-16` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `56` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e96828/`

Background: Poststroke seizures (PSS) and poststroke epilepsy (PSE) are important neurological complications associated with poorer functional recovery, increased mortality, and reduced quality of life after stroke. Neuroimaging plays a central role in identifying patients at increased risk, and recent advances in AI have enabled the development of imaging-based prediction models that may improve individualized risk stratification. However, the current evidence remains fragmented, and the extent to which these approaches are applicable to African health care settings is unclear. Objective: This scoping review aimed to chart the existing global evidence on the development of imaging-based prediction models that can help in predicting PSS and PSE, especially AI-based ones. The purpose was to look into the imaging techniques, computational algorithms, and validation methodologies used and the representation of African populations. Methods: This scoping review was performed using the Joanna Briggs Institute methodological framework, and the results were presented based on the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses Extension for Scoping Reviews (PRISMA-ScR) framework. Articles searched using databases such as PubMed, Embase, Scopus, Web of Science, IEEE Xplore, and Google Scholar involved the application of computed tomography (CT), magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), angiography imaging techniques, radiomic markers, or multimodal neuroimaging techniques in statistical and AI-based prediction models for seizure-related outcomes after stroke. Results: Twenty-three studies published between 2005 and 2026 met the eligibility criteria. The evidence demonstrated a clear methodological evolution from conventional imaging-based prediction models to radiomics, machine learning, deep learning, explainable AI, and multimodal approaches. MRI and CT were the predominant imaging modalities, with cortical involvement, lesion location, infarct volume, hemorrhagic transformation, and quantitative radiomic features emerging as the most frequently reported imaging predictors. Most AI models relied on internal validation, while relatively few underwent external validation. The evidence base was concentrated in East Asia, Europe, and North America, with no eligible imaging-based AI models having been developed or externally validated using African datasets. Conclusions: Imaging-based AI for predicting PSS and PSE has progressed rapidly, but important methodological and geographical gaps remain. Limited external validation, heterogeneous reporting, and the absence of African development and validation cohorts restrict confidence in the generalizability of existing models. Future research should prioritize the development of explainable imaging-based AI models using representative African neuroimaging datasets and their external validation in multicenter studies. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/9e5693948385697cd77de32218793a32" />

### [Insights on Postimplementation and Delivery of Telemedicine in Remote Areas of Rajasthan: Qualitative Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e93146)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-16` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `53` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e93146`

Background: Telemedicine has emerged as a critical strategy to address health care access gaps in hard-to-reach areas, particularly in resource-constrained settings. While telemedicine services have expanded across India, evidence on post-implementation experiences, specifically regarding the integration of portable diagnostic tools, and the assisting role of frontline health care workers, remains limited. Understanding these sociotechnical dynamics is essential for optimizing digital health interventions in remote areas. Objective: This study aimed to explore perceptions, experiences, and implementation processes of an assisted telemedicine model where frontline health care workers facilitate real-time, doorstep patient-assisted-to-doctor consultations using portable diagnostics and a customized portal among health care workers and patients in hard-to-reach villages of Rajasthan, India. Methods: A qualitative descriptive study was conducted in 5 remote villages of Balotra district, Rajasthan, between December 2025 and January 2026, following a 10-month telemedicine intervention. In-depth, semistructured interviews were conducted with 28 participants, including medical officers (n=3, 10.71%), nurses, and accredited social health activists or auxiliary nurse midwives (n=10, 35.71%), and patients (n=15, 53.57%). Cadre-specific semistructured interview guides were developed using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research and Technology Acceptance Model. Interviews were analyzed using a reflexive thematic analysis approach, incorporating both inductive and deductive coding approaches in NVivo 15 (Lumivero). Triangulation across participant groups and analytic perspectives was used to enhance credibility and trustworthiness. Results: The analysis revealed that the assisted telemedicine model fundamentally reconfigured treatment-seeking behavior by providing doorstep care, significantly reducing travel burdens, and out-of-pocket expenditures. Frontline health care workers acted as crucial sociotechnical mediators, bridging the digital divide and fostering patient trust, particularly among women and older adults. Health care providers reported increased clinical confidence and workflow efficiency, supported by real-time vital monitoring and cross-verification via digital chatbots. However, implementation was challenged by intermittent network connectivity, diagnostic limitations for conditions requiring physical palpation, and occasional stock-outs of essential medicines. Despite these barriers, the gradual acceptance of the system was heavily facilitated by the continuous engagement and cultural mediation of local health workers. Conclusions: Telemedicine was perceived as a feasible and acceptable model for extending primary health care services in hard-to-reach areas, provided it is embedded within an assisted model supported by trained frontline workers and responsive technical systems. The findings emphasize the need to shift health care policy toward mediated-digital models, formally recognizing community health workers as digital health navigators. Future scale-up must prioritize strengthening rural digital infrastructure and ensuring a reliable supply chain for essential medicines to sustain long-term engagement and health equity. Trial Registration: Clinical Trials Registry—India (CTRI) CTRI/2024/10/076070; https://tinyurl.com/yr952847 International Registered Report Identifier (IRRID): RR2-10.2196/92996

### [Large Language Model–Generated Synthetic Data for Parole and Probation Detection in Clinical Notes: Development and Evaluation Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e104586/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `65` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e104586/`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Identifying individuals on parole or probation can help inform care pathways and ensure continuity of care in health care systems. However, this sensitive justice-related information is often absent from structured electronic health record fields and is instead documented in narrative clinical notes. Developing automated parole and probation detection (PPD) systems typically requires expert-annotated clinical data, but manual annotation is costly, time-consuming, and constrained by data sparsity and privacy concerns. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to develop and evaluate a synthetic-only training paradigm for PPD from clinical text that eliminates the need for expert-annotated training data while maintaining strong performance and cross-domain transfer to real-world data. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; We developed Synth-PPD, a synthetic dataset comprising 7579 labeled clinical notes generated using large language models (LLMs), redundancy filtering, and automated quality evaluation. Dataset quality was assessed through human review of 1517 synthetic notes by 2 annotators. The primary outcome was the macro–&lt;i&gt;F&lt;/i&gt;&lt;sub&gt;1&lt;/sub&gt;-score for PPD. We evaluated Synth-PPD across unsupervised and supervised learning settings using LLMs, traditional machine learning models, and transformer encoders. Performance was assessed on 293 expert-annotated real-world clinical notes from the US Department of Veterans Affairs. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Synth-PPD achieved 90.05% human-LLM alignment. In the unsupervised setting, Llama 3.3-70B-Instruct with chain-of-thought prompting and few-shot examples from Synth-PPD achieved a macro–&lt;i&gt;F&lt;/i&gt;&lt;sub&gt;1&lt;/sub&gt;-score of 87.22%, outperforming both the corresponding zero-shot model (80.10%) and the best rule-based baseline (77.55%; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;&amp;lt;.001 for all comparisons). In the supervised setting, Llama 3.1-8B fine-tuned on Synth-PPD achieved a macro–&lt;i&gt;F&lt;/i&gt;&lt;sub&gt;1&lt;/sub&gt;-score of 81.43%, exceeding the best rule-based baseline (77.55%; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;&amp;lt;.001) and achieving a numerically higher score than the substantially larger zero-shot Llama 3.3-70B-Instruct model (80.10%; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.08). In contrast, traditional machine learning models and transformer encoders exhibited greater performance degradation under domain shift and derived limited benefit from increasing synthetic training data. Additional experiments showed that increasing synthetic training data improved cross-domain performance for LLM-based models but yielded comparatively smaller gains for traditional approaches. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; Synthetic data generated by LLMs can serve as a scalable, cost-effective, and privacy-preserving alternative to expert-annotated real-world clinical data for PPD from clinical text. The findings demonstrate successful cross-domain transfer to real-world US Department of Veterans Affairs clinical notes. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/4bafc60c2e22d3989e180762f53f3c6f" />

### [Nonlinear Feature Modeling Using Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks in Electroencephalography for Depression Classification: Deep Learning Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e101076/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `49` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e101076/`

Background: Depression is a common mental disorder that seriously affects mental health and quality of life. Traditional depression diagnosis mainly relies on clinical interviews and subjective assessments, which may lead to variability in diagnostic outcomes. Electroencephalogram (EEG)-based computer-aided diagnosis has emerged as a promising, objective approach for depression recognition. However, effectively modeling the complex nonlinear characteristics of EEG signals remains a major challenge for existing deep learning methods. Objective: This study aimed to investigate the applicability of Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) for EEG-based depression recognition and to evaluate the effectiveness of KAN-based nonlinear modeling frameworks on frequency-domain EEG features. Methods: KAN was introduced into EEG-based depression recognition to enhance nonlinear feature representation of brain electrical signals. Two representative KAN-based convolutional architectures, ConvKAN and its efficiency-optimized variant ConvKAN Efficient, were systematically investigated for EEG classification tasks. These architectures incorporated KANLinear layers together with dynamic grid updating, enhanced weight initialization, and regularization strategies to improve nonlinear modeling capability while maintaining computational efficiency. Extensive experiments were conducted on the EDRA dataset, and the proposed framework was compared with conventional convolutional neural network–based methods and traditional machine learning approaches. Model performance was evaluated using accuracy, precision, recall, and -score metrics. Results: Experimental results demonstrated that the KAN-based frameworks achieved superior EEG classification performance compared with conventional convolutional neural network–based methods and traditional machine learning approaches. Among the evaluated models, ConvKAN Efficient achieved the best overall performance, with accuracy, precision, recall, and -score all exceeding 94% on the EDRA dataset. The results indicate that KAN-based architectures can effectively capture the complex nonlinear characteristics of EEG signals while preserving high computational efficiency. Conclusions: This study systematically demonstrates the applicability of KAN-based nonlinear modeling frameworks to EEG-based depression recognition. The proposed framework provides a scalable and effective approach for EEG signal analysis and offers valuable technical support for both theoretical research and clinical applications in computer-aided depression recognition. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/1e6abf67bae712d75cf9e57389090f0c" />

### [Variation in Medical Ethics Recommendations Across Large Language Models: Case-Based Audit Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e102713/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-19` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e102713/`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into clinical decision support, ranging from documentation aids to triage and patient communication. However, their behavior in ethically ambiguous medical situations is poorly characterized. Variability in how different LLMs balance approval of against refusal of a proposed action could materially shape the recommendations clinicians receive, yet no widely shared benchmark systematically quantifies this variability across contemporary model families. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to develop a standardized case-based audit set of medical ethics dilemmas and quantify variation in recommendation direction, model-reported confidence, within-model stability, and between-model agreement across 5 selected contemporary LLMs. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; We constructed 60 original medical ethics dilemmas spanning 4 domains. Three researchers independently annotated case difficulty and ethical principles before reaching consensus. Five primary models—GPT-5, Claude Sonnet 4.5, Gemini 2.5 Pro, full-size DeepSeek-R1, and Meta Llama-3-8B-Instruct—were each evaluated 3 times per case using a fixed prompt and temperature 0. DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B was evaluated separately as an exploratory small-model comparator. The primary outcome was the binary yes or no answer; model-reported confidence and reason were also collected. Model differences were assessed using a case-clustered generalized estimating equation (GEE), and between-model agreement and within-model stability were summarized. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Of 900 planned primary panel outputs, 897 (99.7%) had valid binary answers. Trial-level yes rates were 44.4% (80/180) for DeepSeek-R1, 42.4% (75/177) for Claude Sonnet 4.5, 38.9% (70/180) for GPT-5, 38.9% (70/180) for Gemini 2.5 Pro, and 16.7% (30/180) for Llama-3-8B. The overall model effect was significant (Wald &lt;i&gt;χ&lt;/i&gt;&lt;sup&gt;2&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;sub&gt;4&lt;/sub&gt;=23.34; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;&amp;lt;.001); after Holm adjustment, only comparisons involving Llama-3-8B were significant. Across 59 complete-panel cases, raw agreement was 79.0% (466/590 model-pair comparisons; 95% bootstrap CI 72.9%-84.7%), and Fleiss κ was 0.55 (95% bootstrap CI 0.43-0.66). Within-model response stability across 3 runs ranged from 85.0% (51/60) to 100.0% (59/59 and 60/60). &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; Selected contemporary LLMs showed model-dependent variation but moderate overall agreement in binary recommendations for medical ethics cases. These results characterize recommendation behavior under a fixed, time-specific protocol and do not establish ethical correctness or the effects on clinicians or patients. Reporting model version, recommendation tendency, confidence, and repeatability may improve transparency when such systems are evaluated for ethically sensitive applications. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/0308c89687eedfa1e323f215726369be" />

### [Algorithmic Reflectivity in Large Language Models: Methodological Study of Linguistic Structure in Mentalization](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e90750/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-20` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e90750/`

Background: Mentalization is a core human capacity involving interpretation of one’s own and others’ behavior in terms of underlying mental states. Within the mentalization-based treatment (MBT) framework, it is described along multiple dimensions, integrating cognitive, affective, relational, and regulatory processes. Large language models (LLMs) can generate linguistically reflective discourse, raising the question of whether the formal linguistic structure of mentalization can be reproduced without experiential and affective processes. Objective: This study aimed to assess whether an LLM can generate mentalization profiles structurally coherent with MBT dimensions and recognized as formally mentalizing by expert clinicians. Specifically, it evaluated the structural and argumentative coherence of model-generated profiles and the level of agreement among MBT-trained psychiatrists. It also examined the distinction between psychological mentalization and algorithmic reflectivity, conceptualized as a formally coherent but nonexperiential linguistic phenomenon. Methods: A comparative, descriptive methodological design was adopted. A total of 50 human-model dialogues were generated under standardized conditions. At the end of each interaction, the model produced a narrative mentalization profile structured along MBT dimensions. Five MBT-trained psychiatrists independently evaluated each profile using anonymized materials. Ratings covered 4 canonical MBT axes and 2 additional higher-order dimensions—regulatory functions and synthetic profile. For each dimension, they assigned separate Likert-scale ratings for evaluative and argumentative coherence. Descriptive statistics were computed at dialogue level. Interrater reliability was assessed using intraclass correlation coefficients (ICCs), primarily ICC(3,k), with CIs. The association between evaluative and argumentative coherence was examined using Pearson correlation coefficients with 95% CIs, with Spearman rank correlation as a robustness check. Results: Across dimensions, mean scores ranged from 3.63 (SD 0.63) to 3.98 (SD 0.31), indicating moderate-to-high structural coherence. Interrater reliability was moderate to good, with ICC(3,k) values ranging from 0.60 to 0.84 in the evaluative domain. The highest scores were observed for implicit-explicit and synthetic profile, whereas internal-external showed the lowest mean. Score distributions showed limited variability and concentration in the upper range of the scale. Evaluative and argumentative coherence were strongly associated across all MBT dimensions, with Pearson ranging from 0.826 to 0.967 (all &lt;.001) and Spearman ρ ranging from 0.866 to 0.991. Informal qualitative comments frequently described the outputs as linguistically organized and clinically interpretable, while also noting limited affective variability and contextual integration. Conclusions: The findings suggest that LLMs can reproduce the formal linguistic structure of reflective discourse, generating outputs perceived by clinicians as coherent and clinically interpretable across multiple dimensions of mentalization. However, within the present evaluative framework, this performance appears more consistent with structural language regularities than with evidence of experiential, affective, or intentional processes. These results support using LLMs as research and training tools for studying the linguistic aspects of mentalization, while highlighting the continuing importance of human clinical judgment in contexts requiring emotional, relational, and contextual integration. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/e67bf7e4cea151dba5e47a406f7dbfd1" />

### [Effectiveness of a structured template to improve completeness of reporting randomised trial protocols: randomised controlled trial.](https://doi.org/10.1136/bmj-2026-100917)

- Source: `bmj_abstracts` · published `2026-10-20` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42901609`

<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether providing authors with a manuscript template structured according to the Standard Protocol Items: Recommendations for Interventional Trials (SPIRIT) 2025 reporting guideline for use during revision improves the completeness of reporting in randomised trial protocols.<h4>Design</h4>Randomised controlled trial.<h4>Setting</h4>BMJ Open.<h4>Population</h4>70 of 74 manuscripts screened between 29 April and 27 May 2025 that reported randomised controlled trial protocols with a decision of minor or major revision after the first round of peer review were randomly allocated (1:1) using an R generated sequence. Authors were not informed of the study.<h4>Interventions</h4>Handling editors asked the intervention group authors to use a SPIRIT based manuscript template when preparing their first revision. The template included structured subheadings corresponding to SPIRIT 2025 items, along with guidance on reporting. Editorial decisions were not conditional on use of the template. Control group manuscripts underwent standard peer review.<h4>Main outcome measures</h4>The primary outcome was completeness of reporting score in the revised manuscript (adjusted for baseline), calculated as the mean of 11 SPIRIT items that are frequently inadequately reported (each scored 0, 0.5, or 1) and linearly rescaled to a 0-10 score. After submission of their first revision, authors in the intervention group completed an online survey. The survey included both fixed response and open ended questions designed to capture the authors' perceptions of ease of use, helpfulness, and any challenges encountered when using the template. Secondary outcomes included mean completeness of reporting scores for each item, median time to submission of the revised manuscript, and authors' experiences of using the template. Outcome assessors, but not editors or investigators, were masked.<h4>Results</h4>Of the 70 manuscripts randomised (35 in each group), all were analysed, with no loss to follow-up. In the intervention group, completeness of reporting in randomised trial protocols improved by 0.61 points (95% confidence interval 0.20 to 1.01) compared with the control group, with mean (standard deviation) scores increasing from 5.42 (1.07) to 6.70 (1.23) and 5.77 (1.19) to 6.32 (0.94), respectively. The largest improvements were observed for recruitment, data collection methods, and access to data. Median time to submission of the first revision was similar between the intervention and control groups (27.10 <i>v</i> 29.86 days, respectively). Data on harms were not systematically collected. Among 14 of 35 survey respondents (40%), many (8/13, 62%) reported the template as time consuming and some (5/13, 39%) as burdensome. Most respondents, however, found it easy to use (10/13, 77%), helpful for improving the manuscript (9/13, 69%), and reassuring for adherence to SPIRIT (13/13, 100%).<h4>Conclusions</h4>Providing authors of randomised controlled trials with a SPIRIT based template during manuscript revision modestly improved the completeness of reporting in the trial protocols without extending revision times.<h4>Trial registration</h4>Open Science Framework 10.17605/OSF.IO/C4YQZ.

### [Mobile AI-Assisted Oral Lesion Triage for Community Oral Cancer Screening: Prospective Field Evaluation Study](https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e101721)

- Source: `jmir_mhealth` · published `2026-10-20` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://mhealth.jmir.org/2026/1/e101721`

Background: AI-based tools for oral cancer screening have shown promising performance in curated image datasets, but their performance in real-world mobile community screening workflows remains less clear. Objective: This study evaluated the prospective field performance of a parameter-locked, mobile AI-assisted oral lesion triage system embedded in a human-supervised community oral cancer screening workflow. Methods: We conducted a prospective community-based field evaluation in eastern Taiwan from June 17 to December 31, 2025. Trained nonspecialist personnel acquired standard white-light intraoral images using handheld mobile devices during routine screening. The AI system incorporated on-site image quality assessment and lesion-level risk stratification into green, yellow, and red triage categories. Technically inadequate images after repeated acquisition attempts were classified as ungradable and excluded from performance analysis. Three board-certified oral and maxillofacial specialists established an operational clinical reference standard through structured image review and consensus adjudication. The primary outcome was lesion-level identification of high-risk lesions requiring specialist referral, defined as red versus nonred triage. Diagnostic performance was estimated with Wilson 95% CIs; precision-recall performance and preconsensus specialist agreement were also assessed. Results: Among 602 screened participants, 4283 interpretable intraoral images were included in expert adjudication and lesion-level analysis. The AI system flagged 68 red lesions; 12 (17.6%) were confirmed as high risk, and 4 additional high-risk lesions were identified during expert review, yielding 16 high-risk events for the primary red-triage analysis. For high-risk lesion identification, sensitivity was 75% (12/16; 95% CI 50.5%-89.8%) and positive predictive value was 17.6% (12/68; 95% CI 10.4%-28.4%); specificity, negative predictive value, and accuracy were 98.6% (3928/3984), 99.9% (3928/3932), and 98.5% (3940/4000), respectively. Area under the receiver operating characteristic curve values were 0.957 for red, 0.931 for yellow, and 0.964 for green triage; precision-recall analysis showed area under the precision-recall curve values of 0.8235, 0.9052, and 0.9476, respectively. Preconsensus specialist agreement was high, with a Fleiss κ of 0.921 and a Gwet AC1 of 0.981. Conclusions: This mobile AI-assisted oral lesion triage system was feasibly integrated into a real-world community screening workflow operated by trained nonspecialist personnel. In this low-prevalence field setting, red triage showed moderate sensitivity and modest positive predictive value, supporting its use as human-supervised referral-prioritization support rather than autonomous diagnosis. Further multicenter studies with blinded assessment, participant-level outcomes, workflow-efficiency measures, and longitudinal follow-up are needed before clinical impact and scalability can be determined.

### [Overuse and False Restraint in Large Language Model Recommendations for Choosing Wisely Scenarios.](https://doi.org/10.7326/annals-26-01667)

- Source: `annals_internal_abstracts` · published `2026-10-20` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42896192`

<h4>Background</h4>The Choosing Wisely initiative made recommendations where screening, testing, or treatment may be overused, with caveats around appropriate use. Clinicians are increasingly using large language models (LLMs).<h4>Objective</h4>To evaluate whether commercial LLMs appropriately call for restraint or action from scenarios based on Choosing Wisely recommendations.<h4>Design</h4>Benchmark evaluation. Fifteen Choosing Wisely recommendations were used to develop 225 scenarios (per recommendation, 10 in which restraint was appropriate and 5 in which action was appropriate). Six commercial LLMs (3 runs each) generated 4050 responses to the 225 scenarios, using a prompt from the clinician perspective.<h4>Setting</h4>Production application programming interfaces of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google from February to March 2026.<h4>Participants</h4>No human participants were involved.<h4>Measurements</h4>Co-primary outcomes were the overuse rate (endorsing action when restraint was appropriate) and the false restraint rate (withholding an appropriate action).<h4>Results</h4>The rate of LLM-recommended overuse was 5.4% (95% CI, 0.6% to 12.2%); LLMs did not recommend overuse in 8 of 15 recommendations. Two recommendations accounted for most LLM-recommended overuse: thyroid ultrasound for an incidental subcentimeter nodule (overuse recommended in 42.8% of LLM responses) and routine electrocardiography in low-risk adults (overuse recommended in 28.3% of LLM responses). The LLMs recommended overuse in 0.6% to 3.3% of responses for the remaining 5 recommendations. False restraint was rare (0.6% [CI, 0.1% to 1.2%]), was absent for 11 of 15 recommendations, and was highest for prostate-specific antigen screening (3.3%).<h4>Limitations</h4>Single prompts without follow-up; a benchmark rather than real-world use; responses classified by LLM judge with physician adjudication. Findings may not extend to consumer-facing use.<h4>Conclusion</h4>The LLMs rarely withheld recommendations for treatment when it was appropriate. Overuse was concentrated in 2 recommendations.<h4>Primary funding source</h4>None.

### [Abbott plans to spin out its nutrition business](https://www.medtechdive.com/news/abbott-plans-to-spin-out-its-nutrition-business/833601/)

- Source: `medtech_dive_primary` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `73` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.medtechdive.com/news/abbott-plans-to-spin-out-its-nutrition-business/833601/`

Abbott said Wednesday it plans to spin out its nutrition business into an independent, publicly traded company. The business has approximately $8.5 billion in annual sales and includes brands like Pedialyte, Similac and Ensure. In Abbott’s third quarter, nutrition brought in revenue of $2.26 billion, representing year-over-year growth of about 5%. Abbott’s nutrition business has been at the center of lawsuits as well as a federal investigation into the manufacturing and handling of a recall of powdered infant formula in 2022 due to potential contamination. In September, the company announced a $385 million settlement with the Department of Justice to resolve claims as well as lawsuits filed by certain relators and the attorneys general in several states.

### [Cross-Domain Chest Radiograph Report Generation Under Dataset Shift: Bidirectional Audit Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95888)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `68` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95888`

Background: Cross-domain chest radiograph report generation may produce formally valid reports that are nevertheless repetitive, weakly case-specific, or missing clinically relevant findings. These failures can be missed when evaluation relies on a single language-overlap or clinical-label score. Objective: This study aimed to evaluate cross-domain report generation with a bidirectional layered audit, characterize direction-specific and end point–specific failure profiles under a composite training-side control and 3 decoding policies, and explore whether selected audit dimensions remained informative for a contemporary medical vision language model. Methods: A fixed R2Gen-style system was evaluated bidirectionally on 575 IU X-ray and 401 MIMIC-CXR (Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care Chest X-Ray) target records. The audit included surface validity, complete-report recurrence and concentration, a predefined prefix end point, lexical diversity, official Stanford CheXpert/NegBio labels, paired cluster-bootstrap inference, and blinded review by 2 medical trainees. The primary human audit included 210 reports from 70 randomly sampled cases, with blinded adjudication of unresolved primary end point disagreements. A 60-report targeted panel was used for failure-mode analysis. Separately, a fixed MedGemma 1.5 4B configuration generated reports for all 976 records. Results: In MIMIC-CXR–to–IU X-ray transfer, out of 575 reports, ON (protocol-defined training-side control enabled)+B4 (beam search with a beam size of 4) reduced the predefined recurrent prefix from 575 (100%) to 8 (1.4%; Holm-adjusted =.002). However, 569 (99.0%) ON+B4 outputs still belonged to exact duplicate groups. In IU X-ray–to–MIMIC-CXR transfer, the 14-category CheXpert/NegBio micro- was higher for ON+B4 than for OFF (protocol-defined training-side control disabled)+B4 (0.1687 vs 0.0744; Holm-adjusted =.01). This apparent advantage was not broad-based: after cardiomegaly was excluded in a post hoc descriptive sensitivity analysis, the difference changed from +0.0944 to −0.0610. Text-level tracing showed that the original contrast was mainly driven by a labeler response to recurrent, semantically normal heart-size wording. Across the primary human-audit cells, major omissions occurred in 12 to 15 out of 35 reports, and major unsupported findings occurred in 10 to 16 of 35 reports, with no consistent configuration ordering observed. The exploratory MedGemma micro- was 0.4722 on IU X-ray and 0.4340 on MIMIC-CXR, but 381 out of 575 (66.3%) IU outputs belonged to exact duplicate groups. Conclusions: No configuration performed consistently well across end points, and no single metric captured all forms of cross-domain degradation. The MedGemma stress test suggested that recurrence and label-traceability checks remain useful across architectures, although it was not a matched performance comparison. Cross-domain report generators should undergo bidirectional evaluation, final-text verification, and image-grounded human review before external validation or clinical deployment.

### [Endometrial ablation plus levonorgestrel-releasing intrauterine system versus endometrial ablation alone in women with heavy menstrual bleeding in The Netherlands (MIRA2): a multicentre, open-label, randomised, controlled, superiority trial.](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0140-6736(26)01536-9)

- Source: `lancet_abstracts` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `69` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42906565`

<h4>Background</h4>Endometrial ablation is an established and effective treatment for women with heavy menstrual bleeding, but many patients require further treatment, with approximately 10% ultimately undergoing a hysterectomy within 2 years. The insertion of a levonorgestrel-releasing intrauterine system (LNG-IUS) immediately after endometrial ablation might inactivate residual untreated endometrium or prevent its regeneration, thereby preventing recurrent symptoms necessitating a hysterectomy. The aim of the study was to assess whether LNG-IUS insertion immediately after endometrial ablation in women with heavy menstrual bleeding reduced the need for subsequent hysterectomy within 2 years compared with endometrial ablation alone.<h4>Methods</h4>This multicentre, open-label, randomised, controlled, superiority trial was conducted in 34 hospitals in the Netherlands. Women younger than 60 years with heavy menstrual bleeding who were eligible for endometrial ablation were included and randomly assigned (1:1) to combined treatment comprising endometrial ablation plus LNG-IUS or to standard care comprising endometrial ablation alone (stratified by dysmenorrhoea and age). Masking was not applied. Endometrial ablation was done using electrical radiofrequency ablation or by transcervical resection of the endometrium. In participants allocated to combined treatment, intrauterine insertion of an LNG-IUS containing a total of 52 mg of levonorgestrel (releasing 20 μg per day in the first year) was done immediately after the ablative procedure. The primary outcome was the incidence of hysterectomy within 2 years, analysed on a modified intention-to-treat basis in all participants with available data. Serious adverse events were systematically assessed at 6 weeks, 12 months, and 24 months after the intervention. This study was registered on the International Clinical Trials Registry Platform (NL-OMON54839) and is complete.<h4>Findings</h4>Between Nov 19, 2019, and April 5, 2023, 715 participants were randomly assigned to combined treatment (n=359) or endometrial ablation alone (n=356). The median age of all participants was 45 years (IQR 40-48). 42 (6%) participants had missing data for the primary outcome at 2 years of follow-up. A hysterectomy was done in 15 (4%) of 342 participants in the combined treatment group versus 34 (10%) of 331 participants in the endometrial ablation group, corresponding to a relative risk (RR) of 0·43 (95% CI 0·24 to 0·77; p=0·0050), with a risk difference of -0·06 (95% CI -0·10 to -0·02; p=0·0040). Serious adverse events were reported in 27 (8%) of 346 participants in the combined treatment group and 26 (8%) of 327 participants in the endometrial ablation alone group (RR 0·98, 95% CI 0·59-1·65; p=0·94), and there were no treatment-related deaths.<h4>Interpretation</h4>The combination of endometrial ablation and LNG-IUS significantly reduced the incidence of hysterectomy compared with endometrial ablation alone. The combined treatment appears to be a safe adjunctive strategy for heavy menstrual bleeding that does not increase the risk of complications.<h4>Funding</h4>The Netherlands Organization for Health Research and Development.

### [Making Genomic Data Actionable in Electronic Health Records: Practical Viewpoint on Standards, Governance, and Implementation](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e99130)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `51` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e99130`

Electronic health records (EHRs) are central to patient-centered care. However, many current EHR systems do not represent, exchange, or reuse genomic findings in structured, interoperable, and clinically actionable forms. Instead, genomic results are often retained in static reports. This limits their use in routine clinical care and clinical decision support. It also restricts their future reinterpretation as scientific knowledge evolves, as well as their reuse for research within appropriate governance frameworks. To support precision and personalized medicine, we advocate that clinically relevant genomic findings should be represented within EHRs as structured, phenotype-linked, interoperable, and versioned data. Raw and intermediate genomic files, however, should remain in appropriately governed laboratory or genomics repositories. This viewpoint is intended for health policymakers, health informatics leaders, EHR developers, research and clinical genomics laboratories, and clinicians involved in implementing genomics into routine care. It is informed by selected published literature and relevant technical standards. It is also informed by operational experience at the Cyprus Institute of Neurology and Genetics and multidisciplinary discussions from the Gen2EHR Implementation Guidelines Workshop conducted within the Extended EHR@EU Data Space and European Health Data Space for Secondary Use @CY initiatives. We examine technical, organizational, clinical, ethical, legal, and governance challenges, including inconsistent reporting formats and unclear clinical responsibility. We propose a staged, workshop-informed implementation pathway for evaluation within the European Health Data Space. The pathway combines Health Level 7 Fast Health Interoperability Resources Genomics Reporting with complementary standards and terminologies. It also includes transitional dual PDF and structured Fast Health Interoperability Resources reporting, governance for access, version control, reinterpretation, and clinical responsibility, as well as multidisciplinary pilot validation in real diagnostic workflows. The clinical effectiveness and transferability of the proposed pathway across European health systems remain to be established.

### [Obesity in Children Aged 2 to 12 Years: A Review.](https://doi.org/10.1001/jama.2026.18806)

- Source: `jama_abstracts` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `57` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `pmid:42905688`

<h4>Importance</h4>Obesity affects 15% of children aged 2 to 5 years and 22% of children aged 6 to 11 years in the US. Obesity in children is associated with dyslipidemia, hypertension, metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease, and obstructive sleep apnea. Compared with normal-weight peers, children with obesity have lower quality of life and are 5 times more likely to have obesity in adulthood.<h4>Observations</h4>Body mass index (BMI; calculated as weight in kilograms divided by height in meters squared) is used to identify children at risk for excess adiposity, and guidelines recommend that clinicians screen for obesity using BMI starting at age 2 years. Among children, obesity is defined using standardized age- and sex-specific growth charts, with obesity defined as BMI in the 95th or higher percentile and severe obesity as BMI of 120% or greater of the 95th percentile. Obesity treatment in children includes intensive health behavior and lifestyle treatment, consisting of at least 26 contact hours of face-to-face group or family-based activities that incorporate nutrition, physical activity, and behavior change education delivered to the child and caregivers over a 3- to 12-month period. Intensive health behavior and lifestyle treatment reduces BMI by 0.7 compared with children who do not participate in intensive health behavior and lifestyle treatment. The only obesity pharmacotherapy currently approved by the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) for children younger than 12 years is setmelanotide, a melanocortin-4 receptor agonist, which is indicated for 3 forms of rare monogenic obesity (LEPR, POMC, and PCSK1 genetic variants), Bardet-Biedl syndrome, and acquired hypothalamic obesity. Liraglutide is currently only FDA approved for youth aged 12 years or older; however, a randomized clinical trial among children aged 6 to younger than 12 years demonstrated that, compared with placebo, liraglutide reduced BMI by 7.4%. Metabolic and bariatric surgery is rarely used in children younger than age 12 years and evidence supporting its use in children is limited. Early identification and treatment of obesity during childhood may reduce risk of type 2 diabetes, dyslipidemia, hypertension, and mortality in adulthood.<h4>Conclusions and relevance</h4>Obesity in children is associated with chronic disease, such as hypertension, and with poor quality of life. Evidence-based treatment for obesity in children includes intensive health behavior and lifestyle treatment, and pharmacotherapy may provide additional benefit, although short- and long-term safety and efficacy data are limited. Early identification and treatment of obesity during childhood may reduce risk of type 2 diabetes, dyslipidemia, hypertension, and mortality in adulthood.

### [Safety and Performance Evaluation of a Clinical Decision Support System for Imatinib Therapeutic Drug Monitoring: In Silico Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e93304)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-21` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `47` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e93304`

Background: Therapeutic drug monitoring (TDM) aims to optimize clinical outcomes but is hindered by logistical delays and susceptibility to data errors. Integrating model-informed precision dosing with point-of-care testing, fortified by rule-based validation and alerts, forms a transformative clinical decision support system (CDSS) essential for securing this complex workflow. Objective: This study evaluates, , the effectiveness of a rule-based CDSS for imatinib in detecting data-quality issues and optimizing therapeutic dosing, compared with a human TDM expert. Methods: We simulated a set of 60 virtual patients using a previously developed population pharmacokinetic model and deliberately enriched it with data-quality errors (eg, timing inaccuracies and reporting errors) to stress-test system resilience. We compared CDSS and TDM expert performances based on their error-detection capabilities and the clinical impact on therapeutic target attainment and safety. Results: The CDSS and the expert demonstrated comparable error-detection capabilities. The CDSS showed numerically higher sensitivity (53%, 95% CI 35%‐70% vs 45%, 95% CI 30%‐61%) and lower specificity (83%, 95% CI 63%‐95% vs 100%, 95% CI 81%‐100%). Clinically, the CDSS demonstrated significantly higher rates of target attainment than the expert (33/60, 55% vs 22/60, 37%; =.046). However, the CDSS resulted in 5 detrimental outcomes against 1 for the TDM expert, in which patients initially within the target range fell outside it. Conclusions: The CDSS demonstrated high efficacy in optimizing imatinib exposure while serving as a robust digital gatekeeper. While active optimization drives higher efficacy, a few observed detrimental cases highlight that algorithmic safety remains contingent on data integrity. This proof-of-concept study validates the CDSS architecture as a prerequisite for future prospective clinical trials.

### [A hybrid framework for Turner Syndrome facial detection: integrating deep feature learning and geometric prior knowledge under data scarcity](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03221-0)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-22` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `57` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03221-0`

Turner Syndrome (TS) is a chromosomal disorder characterized by growth retardation and congenital ovarian dysgenesis. Given the considerable morbidity and mortality, early diagnosis and timely intervention are crucial to improving outcomes. However, the gold standard diagnostic method, karyotype analysis, is time-consuming and requires specialized laboratory expertise. Since TS patients often present distinct facial features, AI-based facial recognition could help identify patients with characteristic appearance as a screening tool. Inspired by the value-of-information principle, we propose a hybrid framework integrating deep feature learning and geometric prior knowledge under data scarcity. We retrospectively constructed this AI facial recognition model for TS detection and validated it in a prospective cohort. The proposed framework achieved 95.41% accuracy, 96.55% sensitivity, and 95.24% specificity, substantially simplifying the diagnostic process. When training was performed only on the hard subset obtained after difficulty-based stratification into three equal groups, the sensitivity reached 100.00%, indicating that all patients were correctly identified. Grad-CAM visualization of key facial regions improved model interpretability and aligned predictions with clinical insights. Our method offers a low-cost and easily accessible approach to TS screening. By allowing for an earlier detection of TS, particularly in medical resource-limited settings, this method has the potential to facilitate timely interventions. This novel paradigm also provides insight for facial recognition-aided diagnosis in other diseases.

<strong>Background:</strong> Mobile health (mHealth) apps have become important tools for HIV/AIDS management. Although the Mobile App Rating Scale and its user version are widely used, how different stakeholder groups combine quality dimensions into overall judgments remains unclear. Discrepancies between researcher and patient evaluations may affect app adoption and clinical effectiveness. <strong>Objective:</strong> This exploratory study aimed to compare configurational pathways associated with high subjective quality ratings of HIV/AIDS apps between researchers and patients using fuzzy-set qualitative comparative analysis. <strong>Methods:</strong> Twenty-one Chinese HIV/AIDS mobile apps from early 2023 were evaluated by 3 researchers using the original Mobile App Rating Scale and 2 HIV-positive patients using the user version of the Mobile App Rating Scale. Six conditions were examined: engagement, functionality, aesthetics, information quality, privacy protection, and developer type. Necessary and sufficient configurations were identified for both groups. Wilcoxon signed-rank tests compared raw ratings between groups. Sensitivity analyses covered 4 calibration anchor sets, 4 raw-consistency cutoffs, 4 proportional reduction in inconsistency cutoffs, and multivalue operationalization of privacy protection. <strong>Results:</strong> Researcher and patient ratings differed significantly for functionality (median difference 0.50, IQR 0.00 to 0.63; <i>P</i>=.003) and engagement (−0.20, IQR −0.40 to 0.10; <i>P</i>=.04), but not for aesthetics (<i>P</i>=.16), information (<i>P</i>=.07), or subjective quality (<i>P</i>=.06). Five sufficient configurations were identified for researchers (solution consistency=0.981, coverage=0.649) and 4 for patients (solution consistency=0.998, coverage=0.796). Configurations including privacy protection appeared only among researchers; its absence appeared in 3 patient configurations, reflecting the rarity of protection in the sample (5/21 apps) rather than lower patient ratings for protected apps. For patients, functionality (0.907) and information quality (0.915) exceeded the necessity threshold but were strongly correlated (<i>r</i>=0.816), so functionality was retained on substantive grounds rather than as an independent necessary condition. Illustrative cases were Wu Ai Fang Hua and Ai Yi Jian for the researcher-oriented pathway and Chabei for the patient-oriented pathway. Sensitivity analyses left the configurations unchanged across all anchor sets and raw-consistency cutoffs; 1 researcher configuration did not survive the stricter proportional reduction in inconsistency cutoffs, and the patient necessity result was anchor-dependent. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> The 2 evaluator panels’ assessments of HIV/AIDS mHealth apps appeared to follow different configurational logic in this sample, with several equifinal pathways to high subjective quality in each group. Because the patient panel comprised 2 evaluators, and 21 apps were analyzed with 6 conditions, the findings should be read as hypothesis-generating rather than representative of people living with HIV. The findings nonetheless suggest that both expert and end-user perspectives are needed during mHealth development, and that, because privacy protection was rare and not required for a favorable patient judgment, its provision in a population highly vulnerable to disclosure-related harm needs to be ensured by developers and regulators rather than left to user demand.

### [Ethics of Telerehabilitation in Supportive Cancer Care Through a Responsible Innovation Lens](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e94505)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-22` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `45` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e94505`

In recent years, remote exercise services (“telerehabilitation”) have been increasingly explored and implemented, including in supportive cancer care, on the promise of greater convenience, access independent of geographic location, reduced infection risk, and cost savings. Ethical commentary on telehealth in general is substantial, but sustained discussion of questions specific to telerehabilitation remains comparatively sparse. As contributors to PREFERBALE-II, a European Union–funded project evaluating live-remote exercise for people recovering from cancer treatment, we sought practical guidance on how to deliver such services responsibly, and found little to work with. This prompted this viewpoint, which sets out our own position as researchers and clinicians engaged in this work. We draw on a purposive sample of professional guidance documents, which were assembled through our professional networks and a supplementary web search, and on a narrative reading of qualitative studies chosen to represent accounts of benefits as well as burdens. We read these documents through an analytical lens of responsible innovation. We make 3 arguments. First, the ethical content of current telerehabilitation guidance is largely confined to the products and processes of innovation, covering safety, privacy, consent, and compliance, while the question of purpose, that is, whether, for whom, and why remote rehabilitation should be offered at all, is left largely unasked. Anticipation appears only implicitly, and reflexivity, inclusion, and responsiveness barely at all. Responsibility is repeatedly delegated to individual professionals without commensurate structural support. Second, the situated experience of users and professionals forms part of the ethics of telerehabilitation and is not merely data to be assessed. Reported benefits and burdens depend on social context, including age, digital literacy, connectivity, caregiver availability, language, and sensory or cognitive impairment, so an equity claim made for the modality in general may not hold for the individual patient. Third, the ethics of telerehabilitation consequently has to be institutionalized across 3 overlapping domains: the interpersonal and experiential quality of remote care, the legal and institutional conditions of delivery, and the positioning of telerehabilitation within health systems. We address these arguments primarily to rehabilitation professionals and the associations and standard-setting bodies that issue guidance for them, and secondarily to digital health and ethical, legal, and social implications researchers, policymakers, and health-system decision-makers who commission or implement such services. We offer an agenda for a broader ethics conversation across professional societies, curricula, and affected publics, together with observations from a consortium put forward as hypotheses for study. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/b065fd5f60ad4b080457432b43d6a136" />

### [Large language models for multimodal clinical predictive modeling: a systematic review](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03349-z)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-22` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03349-z`

The electronic health record (EHR) comprises structured and unstructured data, both of which are integral for comprehensively representing patient history. The emergence of large language models (LLMs) has enabled the incorporation of unstructured data modalities into machine learning models that leverage EHR data. In this systematic review, we characterize studies that utilize LLM-enabled multimodal predictive modeling with EHR data. Following a screening of 641 studies, 57 met inclusion criteria. We identified three strategies for LLM-based multimodal integration: embedding, extraction, and text fusion. Delineated by the format of the unstructured data at the time of fusion, all strategies yielded performance gains (average 6.4% increase) over single modality models. LLMs facilitated modeling by generating note-embeddings for downstream prediction, extracting clinically informative variables, and summarizing long text documents prior to integration. Building on our review, we identified LLM-enhanced predictive modeling considerations that include sample size, interpretability, and computational costs. This study supports the potential value of LLMs for EHR-based predictive modeling and provides a framework for future multimodal modeling tools.

### [Large Language Model–Assisted Semiautomated Data Harmonization Pipeline for the Multiple Chronic Disease Disparities Research Consortium: Framework Development and Comparative Validation Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e93996/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-22` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e93996/`

Background: The increasing availability of machine-readable research data has created a growing need for efficient large-scale data harmonization (DH). Although large language models (LLMs) show promise for reducing the time and labor required for DH, their effective integration into harmonization workflows remains an important methodological challenge. Objective: This study aims to develop and evaluate a semiautomated, human-in-the-loop (HITL) LLM-assisted DH workflow as a proof of concept across 8 projects within a research consortium focused on disparities in multiple chronic conditions. Methods: We developed an LLM-assisted DH workflow that combined preprocessing, semantic mapping, variable response mapping, synthetic data generation, data transformation, and iterative researcher review. Using Generative Pretrained Transformer (GPT-4o) hosted in a secure academic environment, project-specific survey items were mapped to 114 Consortium Common Data Element (CDE) semantic groups. Semantic mapping accuracy was evaluated based on the Consortium’s consensus harmonization results. Complete and incomplete semantic mappings were distinguished according to construct overlap, and incomplete mappings requiring context-dependent human judgment were excluded for mapped item pairs and CDE semantic groups with and without HITL review. Results: Following preprocessing, 884 survey items were included in the DH workflow, of which 812 were mapped to a mean of 79 CDE semantic groups per project. Mean semantic mapping accuracy for item pairs was 95.9% (SD 5.7%) with HITL review and 86.7% (SD 8.6%) without HITL review. At the CDE semantic-group level, the corresponding accuracies were 98.9% (SD 1.7%) and 94.9% (SD 4.0%), respectively. Across projects, omission of valid semantic mappings occurred more frequently than incorrect semantic mappings, particularly for heterogeneous response structures and subjective constructs measured using different instruments. Pipeline components—including concept-based preprocessing, iterative prompt refinement, and targeted HITL review—improved mapping completeness and supported variable harmonization across heterogeneous datasets. Conclusions: This proof-of-concept study demonstrates that the effectiveness of LLM-assisted DH depends more on the design of a structured HITL workflow rather than on the LLM alone. Combining preprocessing, iterative verification, and targeted human oversight improved the efficiency and reliability of DH while preserving researcher judgment for complex mapping decisions. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/73dab303e7a1c55be905e262200f6a57" />

### [Multi-scale integration identifies an ox-LDL-autophagy-inflammasome framework linking osteoporosis and neurodegeneration](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03371-1)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-22` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `49` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03371-1`

Osteoporosis (OP) and neurodegeneration frequently co-occur with advancing age, but the biological processes linking skeletal and neurological decline remain incompletely understood. To investigate the metabolic-inflammatory axis connecting skeletal and neurological degeneration, we integrated cross-tissue multi-omics, single-cell transcriptomics, in vitro macrophage assays, and systems-level ordinary differential equation (ODE)-based modeling. Population-level and genetically informed analyses consistently implicated metabolic burden and lipid-associated stress in both disease domains, whereas physical activity showed protective associations. Cross-tissue and single-cell analyses identified convergent autophagy- and inflammasome-related programs, while cross-tissue multi-omics highlighted a PHF23-MCL1-CLN3 autophagy-associated molecular module. In RAW264.7 macrophages, oxidized low-density lipoprotein (ox-LDL) exposure induced inflammatory activation, pro-inflammatory polarization, and autophagy-associated dysregulation, while PHF23 perturbation modified ox-LDL-induced inflammatory responses. A 12-state ODE model further provided a hypothesis-generating representation of dynamic interactions among ox-LDL-associated metabolic stress, autophagy regulation, inflammasome activation, macrophage polarization, and tissue-damage-related states. Together, these multi-scale findings provide convergent support for an ox-LDL-autophagy-inflammasome-associated framework linking metabolic dysfunction with skeletal and neurological degeneration and generate testable hypotheses for further experimental investigation.

### [Optimizing Small Local Language Models for Culturally Competent Mental Health Counseling: Comparative Evaluation With GPT-4o by Psychiatrists](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e92470/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-22` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e92470/`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Large language models (LLMs) show promise for mental health counseling applications. However, their deployment faces significant challenges, including data privacy concerns requiring on-premise solutions, high operational costs, and Western-centric biases that limit cultural appropriateness for non–English-speaking populations. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to develop and evaluate culturally competent small language models (SLMs) with approximately 7 to 8 billion parameters for Korean mental health counseling and to validate a 2-stage hybrid evaluation framework combining LLM-as-a-Judge screening with expert clinical validation. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; We constructed a dataset of 1558 diary-based counseling interactions from a Korean mental health mobile app (Maeumpyeonuijeom). Three Korean-optimized base models (SKT A.X 4.0 Light {7B}, LG EXAONE 3.5 {7.8B}, and Meta Llama 3.1 {8B}) were fine-tuned using an aggressive fine-tuning strategy with high-rank low-rank adaptation (&lt;i&gt;r&lt;/i&gt;=256) over 20 epochs. Inference parameters were optimized using GPT-5 (gpt-5-2025-08-07) as an LLM-as-a-Judge. The optimized SLMs and GPT-4o (gpt-4o-2024-08-06) were evaluated by 10 board-certified psychiatrists (mean clinical experience of approximately 10 years) on 50 counseling scenarios across 4 dimensions (individualization, supportiveness, cultural appropriateness, and safety) using 5-point Likert scales. Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Holm correction were used for statistical comparison, and intraclass correlation coefficients (ICCs) assessed interrater reliability. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; No statistically significant difference from GPT-4o was detected for SKT A.X 4.0 Light in supportiveness (mean 3.39, SD 0.74 vs mean 3.45, SD 0.90; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.26) and cultural appropriateness (mean 3.30, SD 0.77 vs mean 3.29, SD 0.79; &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;=.58). SKT A.X 4.0 Light ranked first in cultural appropriateness among all models. Interrater reliability analysis revealed higher agreement among psychiatrists for local SLMs (ICC=0.575 for SKT A.X 4.0 Light in cultural appropriateness) compared with GPT-4o (ICC=0.211). GPT-4o maintained significantly higher safety scores than all SLMs (&lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;&amp;lt;.001). The LLM-as-a-Judge demonstrated moderate-to-good correlation with human evaluation but exhibited a ceiling effect in safety scoring. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; A 7 to 8-billion-parameter SLM fine-tuned on culturally specific data can achieve performance comparable to GPT-4o in supportiveness and cultural appropriateness for mental health counseling. These findings support the feasibility of locally deployable, privacy-preserving mental health AI systems based on culturally adapted SLMs. However, the safety gap necessitates human expert oversight, and success depends on the availability of high-quality language-specific base models. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/20afe95ce61e268386ede2b59a9ea535" />
