### [Reducing Annotation Costs Using Knowledge Distillation for Label‑Efficient Negation Detection in Radiology Reports: Multisite Internal Validation Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e103912)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-22` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `61` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e103912`

<strong>Background:</strong> Negation in radiology reports (eg, “no evidence of dissection”) is essential for accurately conveying diagnostic information. Clinical natural language processing systems, however, often fail to determine whether findings occur within negated or speculative contexts, leading to excessive false keyword alerts, noise in information extraction, and reduced precision in downstream clinical or research applications. Transformer models have advanced detection capabilities but require costly expert labeling. Large language models (LLMs) may provide label-efficient alternatives through few-shot prompting or knowledge distillation. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study aims to systematically evaluate LLMs and transformer models under different supervision strategies, including comparisons of expert-labeled and LLM-distilled transformers, teacher LLMs (GPT-5.3 Chat, Gemma 4, and Qwen3.6-Plus), sampling approaches, and direct few-shot LLM inference, to determine whether LLM inference or LLM-based distillation can substitute for human labeling in radiology-report negation detection while reducing annotation costs. <strong>Methods:</strong> We reused and analyzed 6000 deidentified English-language radiology reports from 3 hospital branches. Three LLMs—GPT-5.3 Chat, Gemma 4, and Qwen3.6-Plus—performed 0-, 5-, and 10-shot inference to identify negations and speculations unrelated to abnormal findings and generate pseudo-labels for transformer fine-tuning (Bio_ClinicalBERT [Bio + Clinical BERT model], BERT [Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers], and ALBERT [A Lite Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers]). We trained models using human-labeled or LLM-distilled supervision across 28 training data scales (n=42-4200) and 3 data sampling seeds and compared them across multiple supervision and training conditions encompassing backbone, label source, and sampling strategy. We assessed performance using keyword-level precision, recall, and F1-score with paired bootstrap tests (10,000 replicates). <strong>Results:</strong> We refined 1000 report annotations (interrater κ=0.957) and fine-tuned 1260 transformer models. Human-labeled transformers consistently outperformed distillation-labeled ones, with the largest gap at small training scales (ΔF1-score ≈0.042; adjusted <i>P</i>&lt;.001), which narrowed at larger scales (ΔF1-score ≈0.022; adjusted <i>P</i>&lt;.001). Teacher model choice had minimal effect on distilled performance. All 3 human-labeled transformer backbones (BERT, ALBERT, and Bio_ClinicalBERT) achieved high keyword-level F1-scores (0.982-0.985 under their best conditions; n=4200). In the 10-shot setting, GPT-5.3 Chat (F1-score 0.974) modestly underperformed human-labeled transformers in the large-sample range (ΔF1-score=0.009; adjusted <i>P</i>=.047) and significantly outperformed Gemma 4 (F1-score 0.957; adjusted <i>P</i>=.01) and Qwen3.6-Plus (F1-score 0.950; adjusted <i>P</i>&lt;.001) in the bootstrap analysis. GPT-5.3 Chat labeled reports at approximately US $0.018 per report and became less expensive than manual annotation when expert hourly wages exceeded US $0.64-1.50. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> Human-supervised transformers remain the strongest baseline, but frontier LLMs such as GPT-5.3 Chat offer comparable performance with minimal supervision. LLM-based distillation offers a cost-effective alternative with only minor performance loss. Dataset scale proved more influential than sampling strategy. Additionally, superior teacher performance yielded no proportional gains through distillation, as open-weight or low-cost teachers achieved comparable results. If supported by external validation, the proposed label-efficient framework could enable accurate and affordable negation detection and rapid domain adaptation.

### [Stress-Symptom Interaction in Inflammatory Bowel Disease: Longitudinal Digital Phenotyping Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95854)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-22` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `53` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e95854`

Background: Stress and inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) symptoms may vary over short time scales, but these dynamics are not well captured by intermittent clinical assessments. Objective: The study aimed to characterize exploratory time-varying stress-symptom patterns using intensive longitudinal monitoring and to evaluate a transparent, patient-clustered analysis framework. Methods: A prospective longitudinal design was used with 74 patients with IBD monitored for 90 days. Daily data comprised 12 core indicators and 2 auxiliary measures. The canonical analysis used patient-level partial-correlation PCMCI (Peter and Clark Momentary Conditional Independence) with a τ of 0 to 2, patient-specific network scaling, a fixed 30-day cycle-end stress contrast, exploratory module-control, and effective-information summaries, and a secondary exploratory time-varying effect model (TVEM). Results: Mean stress was higher during cycle days 26 to 30 (mean 4.789, SD 1.079) than during nonwindow days (mean 4.160, SD 1.013), with a mean within-person difference of 0.629 (95% CI 0.550‐0.708; <.001); 71 of 74 (95.9%) participants showed a positive difference. Symptom scores had the highest mean of 9.88 (SD 0.09) and lowest dispersion, whereas stressors showed greater variability (mean 3.68, SD 0.49). The estimated network showed predominant forward connections from psychosocial factors through emotion regulation and psychophysiological amplification indicators to gut-specific symptoms. The psychophysiological amplification module had the highest micro-to-macro flow efficiency and macro-level control metric. In the TVEM analysis, the full module-control and causal-emergence models explained 64.4% and 45.6% of the deviance, respectively. Conclusions: In this longitudinal IBD patient cohort, daily monitoring identified a reproducible end-of-cycle stress comparison and an exploratory multiscale organization that links psychosocial, emotional, psychophysiological, and gut-specific symptom domains. The psychophysiological amplification module provides the most informative, comprehensive representation, although its organization varies depending on follow-up and disease status. These findings support the use of digital phenotypes to generate clinical evidence for the coordination of stress symptoms, tailored to individual situations.

### [A deep learning framework for automated cervical spinal cord vertebral labeling and morphometric extraction from brain MRI with large-scale multicenter validation](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03403-w)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `69` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03403-w`

This study aimed to develop and validate a fully automated pipeline for vertebral level identification (C1–C3) of the upper cervical spinal cord (SC) in T1-weighted brain MRI with a quality control module to assess the generated masks. MRI data from 998 controls and 879 patients with hereditary ataxias (32 sites; age 5–85 years; 46% male) were used to train deep learning models (nnU-Net, U-Net with deep supervision, SwinUNETR) for automatic vertebral labeling of C1–C3 levels. A secondary model based on VGG, ResNet, and EfficientNet architectures was trained to identify vertebral labeling errors. The pipeline also generates image quality metrics including signal-to-noise ratio, contrast-to-noise ratio, coefficient of joint variation, and entropy focus criterion. External validation was performed using 2737 MRIs from four public datasets, and clinical utility was demonstrated in a longitudinal cohort of 51 patients with multiple sclerosis. The nnU-Net achieved the highest performance (Dice: C1 = 0.85, C2 = 0.89, C3 = 0.89), labeling 95.8% of mislabeled cases from the Spinal Cord Toolbox. The VGG-based classifier achieved 97.9% accuracy and 98.7% sensitivity. External validation showed labeling accuracies exceeding 92% in four of five datasets and sensitivity to longitudinal SC atrophy in multiple sclerosis. This pipeline enables scalable, reliable vertebral labeling and morphometric extraction of the upper cervical SC for multicenter studies and supports biomarker development in neurodegenerative diseases.

### [Autologous erythrocyte microparticles drive antitumour T cell immunity via haem-mediated dendritic cell activation](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01795-4)

- Source: `nature_biomedical_engineering` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `66` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01795-4`

Current vaccine adjuvants, such as aluminum salts and emulsions, are limited by weak induction of cytotoxic T cell immunity and restricted ability to promote T helper type 1 responses, highlighting the need for safer and more effective alternatives. Here we show that microparticles generated from erythrocytes by controlled ultraviolet irradiation function as potent T helper type 1-skewing adjuvants that enhance tumour antigen-specific cytotoxic T cell responses in vitro and in multiple mouse tumour models. Following uptake by dendritic cells, these microparticles traffic to lysosomes and deliver haem cargo that is subsequently transported to the nucleus. Mechanistically, the particles elevate lysosomal pH, promoting lysosome movement towards the nucleus and enabling haem transfer, where haem activates REV-ERB⍺ and forms a co-repressor complex that suppresses peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor gamma, thereby relieving inhibition of dendritic cell maturation and antigen presentation. In vaccination settings, combining tumour antigens with erythrocyte-derived microparticles enhances cytotoxic T cell activation and suppresses tumour growth with minimal toxicity. These findings establish host-derived vesicles as a promising platform for next-generation cancer vaccine adjuvants. Microparticles generated from erythrocytes by controlled ultraviolet irradiation act as T helper type 1-skewing adjuvants that enhance tumour antigen-specific cytotoxic T cell responses in vitro and across mouse tumour models.

### [Bispecific dual T cell agonist invikafusp alfa in solid tumors: the phase 1/2 START-001 trial](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04711-0)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `61` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04711-0`

Invikafusp alfa (invika), a first-in-class, bispecific, dual T cell agonistic antibody selectively targeting Vβ6/Vβ10 T cell subsets, is being evaluated in START-001, a multicenter phase 1/2 monotherapy trial in participants with mostly immune checkpoint blockade (ICB)-resistant, antigen-rich solid tumors. Primary outcomes were safety/tolerability and recommended phase 2 dose (RP2D) in phase 1 and anti-tumor activity in phase 2. As of 12 March 2025, phase 1 completed dose escalation (n = 34) through dose levels of 0.01, 0.02, 0.04, 0.08, 0.12 and 0.16 mg kg−1 without reaching maximum tolerated dose, and initial phase 2 enrolled 18 participants. Two participants experienced reversible dose-limiting toxicities, one at each of the 0.08 mg kg−1 and 0.16 mg kg−1 dose levels. The most common treatment-related adverse event was cytokine release syndrome, mainly grades 1 and 2. No treatment-related deaths or non-laboratory grade 4 adverse events were reported. At the optimal biological doses (OBDs; 0.08 mg kg−1 and 0.12 mg kg−1) in combined phase 1 and phase 2 cohorts, the overall objective response rate was 14.8% (4/27), including confirmed partial responses in immunologically cold tumors, such as microsatellite stable colorectal cancer and programmed death-ligand 1 (PD-L1)-negative non-small cell lung cancer. No objective responses were seen at doses lower than 0.08 mg kg−1. Invika selectively and potently expanded peripheral and intratumoral Vβ6/Vβ10 T cells, of a predominantly memory-like phenotype, dose-dependently, peaking at the OBDs. Pharmacodynamic increases in interferon-γ and soluble CD25 followed a similar pattern. Integrated analyses from safety, pharmacokinetic, pharmacodynamic and preliminary anti-tumor data supported the selection of 0.08 mg kg−1 as the RP2D. In conclusion, phase 1 of START-001 met its primary outcomes by demonstrating the acceptable safety profile of invika consistent with its mechanism of action and selecting an RP2D. Initial phase 2 results further corroborated the safety of invika and expanded its preliminary evidence of clinically meaningful anti-tumor activity in ICB-resistant solid tumors, supporting further evaluation in the ongoing phase 2. ClinicalTrials.gov identifier: NCT05592626 . As presented at the European Society for Medical Oncology Congress 2026, in a phase 1/2 trial in participants with solid tumors, invika, a first-in-class bispecific agonistic antibody that selectively targets the prevalent Vβ6/Vβ10 T cell subsets, was well tolerated, enabling the selection of a recommended phase 2 dose for further testing, and showed a preliminary efficacy signal in both immunologically hot and cold tumors.

### [Clinical AI governance for scalable innovation in value based healthcare systems](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03409-4)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `54` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03409-4`

Health systems are deploying clinical artificial intelligence (AI) faster than they can govern it, and existing frameworks emphasize risk oversight over operational learning and scale. The Collaborative Technology Adoption for Care Transformation (C-TACT) framework, developed at Southern California Permanente Medical Group (SCPMG), organizes governance across three pillars (People, Process, Technology) and four lifecycle stages (Vendor Evaluation, Pilot, Scale, Maintenance), each operationalized through a domain-weighted scorecard. Scale and Maintenance scorecards incorporate business and operational execution as weighted domains, with Analytic Hierarchy Process methods (consistency ratio 0.0037). In an illustrative, retrospective application, the Pilot Score Card separated two published SCPMG AI deployments and four anonymized comparators (47.0–53.5) by approximately thirty points across three weighting schemes. This separation illustrates how the framework behaves in this comparison. It is not evidence of validated discriminative performance, because the comparator scores were reconstructed retrospectively and were not scored under blinded conditions. C-TACT is presented here as a governance framework and a methodological demonstration. Prospective, blinded validation is needed to establish its discriminative and predictive performance under real-world conditions.

### [Data-Rich But Integration-Poor Human Trials: Conceptual Framework for Closing the AI Transformation Gap in Dietary Intervention and Cosmetic Human Trials](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e103706)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `33` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e103706`

Human trials in dietary and cosmetic domains generate large volumes of multimodal data but remain limited in their ability to produce integrated and actionable insights. Data such as intake records, physiological biomarkers, imaging outputs, and user-reported outcomes are typically collected through disconnected systems and analyzed independently, restricting the ability to establish time-resolved relationships between exposure and response. In this viewpoint, we define this limitation as the AI transformation (AX) gap, representing a structural disconnect between data generation and actionable knowledge in human trials. Drawing on evidence from real-world industry demand, we highlight how this gap is consistently observed across dietary intervention trials, cosmetic human trials, and underlying data systems. We propose a conceptual AX-driven framework that integrates multimodal data acquisition, continuously aligns intervention exposure with corresponding physiological, phenotypic, and participant-reported responses across time, and supports centralized analytical processing. The framework is intended to provide a unified perspective for transforming fragmented human-trial data into integrated, interpretable, and actionable knowledge. Although the proposed framework has not yet been empirically validated, it offers a conceptual foundation for future development and evaluation of integrated data platforms in dietary intervention and cosmetic human trials. Closing the AX gap may facilitate the transition from fragmented data collection toward human trials in which multimodal exposure and response data are continuously linked across time to support more integrated and individualized interpretation in research and product development. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/d1f6e8afa19d7560faef3dd3bb9a24ba" />

### [Deriving a Deployment Threshold for Benefit and Harm in Clinical AI: Multi-Institutional Decision-Analytic Modeling Study](https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e109863/)

- Source: `jmir_ai` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `66` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://ai.jmir.org/2026/1/e109863/`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Clinical AI operates in a health care environment heterogeneous in biology, clinical practice, and documentation, so its benefit-harm balance is deployment-specific: an intervention can help in one setting and harm in another when the determinant of response is not routinely observed. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study had 2 aims: to derive the deployment threshold at which an AI intervention whose effect changes sign becomes beneficial, using zero-phase component analysis whitening (a retraining-free correction to clinical retrieval embeddings) as a worked case, and to determine whether the evidence needed to apply that threshold exists in the published literature or must be measured locally. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; We specified a 3-arm decision-analytic model—no action, universal deployment, and screen-and-treat—for interventions with sign-changing effects and instantiated it on a retraining-free correction to clinical retrieval embeddings. Empirical effect groups were defined by measured response rather than nominal mechanistic tier. Screening accuracy was derived from between-condition variability and tested by local resampling. Transportability was evaluated on emergency-department notes and discharge summaries from 2 US academic centers. A structural sensitivity analysis allowed the downstream value of benefit and harm to differ. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Measured response defined 6 benefited and 7 harmed configurations at the base-case regularization. The universal-deployment threshold was &lt;i&gt;p&lt;/i&gt;*=0.50; the exact and zero-cost forms differ by 2.8×10&lt;sup&gt;–5&lt;/sup&gt;, so strategy cost had a negligible effect on it, and across 6 regularization settings, the threshold ranged from 0.32 to 0.70. Published descriptions did not identify the response-defined state: none of the 55 published systems had been evaluated under the intervention, so response prevalence remained unidentified without classification assumptions. Local screening reduced the minimum required prior probability from 0.210 with 1 scored condition to 0.071 with 6. The nominal mechanistic tier agreed with response in all 52 institutional section-level cells, but 1 benchmark-harmed configuration benefited in all 4 institutional conditions. In the joint fit-and-evaluation resampling protocol, specificity rose from 0.328 at 10 documents to 0.974 at 75 documents. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; The deployment threshold is analytically identifiable, but the state needed to apply; it cannot be recovered reliably from model identity or the published literature. Deployment validity is therefore local in the setting examined: local measurement is needed when the response-defining state cannot otherwise be established reliably before deployment, and an unsuitable measured state requires local action rather than plug-and-play transfer. The data do not identify any single adaptation method as universally necessary. &lt;strong&gt;Trial Registration:&lt;/strong&gt; <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/78fba0a4f5a38d7e2cda6240253399df" />

### [Digital Archiving of Direct-to-Consumer Marketing Claims Made by Businesses Selling Unproven Stem Cell Interventions](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95156)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `48` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e95156`

Over the last 2 decades, a large global marketplace has emerged in which businesses use direct-to-consumer advertising to sell putative stem cell treatments that are unsupported by convincing scientific evidence of safety and efficacy. It is common for such purported stem cell therapies not to have been reviewed and approved by national regulatory authorities, even though they are supposed to undergo rigorous premarketing review by regulatory bodies responsible for evaluating such medical products and determining whether they are backed by sufficient evidence to warrant approval. The marketing claims, commercial activities, and clinical practices of such businesses have resulted in patients experiencing serious harms, including loss of life, physical injuries, financial losses, and avoidable delays in accessing evidence-based treatments. Such businesses are also responsible for collective harms, including diverting patients from participating in legitimate stem cell clinical studies, damaging the field of credible stem cell research, and contributing to public confusion and misunderstandings about how to distinguish evidence-based stem cell therapies from stem cell interventions that are not backed by robust data. Researchers in bioethics, health law, the social sciences, and other academic fields have made important contributions to the critical analysis of direct-to-consumer digital marketing claims made by businesses selling unproven and unapproved stem cell interventions. Scholars studying such marketing representations have documented the use of persuasive “tokens of legitimacy” to make advertised stem cell interventions seem credible, trustworthy, and backed by current scientific evidence. Acknowledging the value of such scholarship, there is a growing need for the development of best practices in research methods and data-archiving practices. In particular, there is a need for increased digital archiving of online marketing claims made by businesses selling supposed stem cell treatments. Numerous parties can benefit from increased digital archiving of such problematic marketing representations. However, analyzing, archiving, and critiquing the marketing claims, business operations, and clinical practices of such businesses must be done in a careful, rigorous, and evidence-based manner that is attentive to the intimidating tactics some of these businesses use when responding to public criticism. Researchers need to document the marketing representations made by businesses selling unapproved and unproven stem cell interventions in ways that minimize exposure to legal threats and other efforts to suppress critical analysis. Using the Wayback Machine or other comparable archiving tools to create digital repositories of such marketing representations is one way to mitigate such threats, by capturing the exact claims such businesses make when promoting putative stem cell therapies. Archiving such digital marketing representations is important for academic research and can play a valuable role in other contexts, such as in legal actions taken against businesses using purportedly deceptive marketing claims to sell unproven and unapproved stem cell-based interventions. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/57750c7523501afd27939aef8ed3b530" />

### [Electrocardiogram Embeddings Beyond Chronological Age for Prediagnostic Cognitive Impairment and Dementia: Development and External Validation Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e108392)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e108392`

&lt;strong&gt;Background:&lt;/strong&gt; Dementia is a growing health burden worldwide; however, scalable, low-cost strategies for early risk identification are lacking. Because cardiac and cognitive aging are closely associated, a routinely collected 12-lead electrocardiogram (ECG), interpreted using foundation model embeddings, is a promising and accessible candidate marker. However, it is unclear whether such embeddings carry predictive information independent of chronological age—the dominant risk factor for dementia—and whether the underlying changes follow a detectable prediagnostic trajectory. &lt;strong&gt;Objective:&lt;/strong&gt; This study aimed to investigate whether foundation model embeddings of routine ECGs carry information that distinguishes patients later diagnosed with cognitive impairment or dementia from matched controls, beyond chronological age, and, if so, whether the underlying ECG changes intensify as diagnosis approaches. &lt;strong&gt;Methods:&lt;/strong&gt; In a retrospective two-site Korean cohort (Severance Hospital, development n=52,355; Yongin Severance Hospital, external n=6500), embeddings from the knowledge-enhanced diagnosis foundation model supported an ECG-only classifier that discriminated patients diagnosed with cognitive impairment or dementia within the following 10 years from matched controls, using only ECGs interpreted as normal sinus rhythm. We compared an age-only model with a late fusion of age and ECG-derived probability and tested whether age-residualized embedding is predictive as direct evidence of age-independent information. Discrimination was summarized by the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) with 95% bootstrap CIs, and DeLong tests and net reclassification improvement (NRI) were used for between-model comparisons. To trace the prediagnostic time course, we analyzed ECG phenotype labels across four windows before diagnosis in an exact-age-matched case-control set. &lt;strong&gt;Results:&lt;/strong&gt; Chronological age strongly discriminated cases from controls in both cohorts (external AUROC 0.809, 95% CI 0.799-0.819). Adding ECG-derived probability improved discrimination, calibration, and net benefit over age alone (external AUROC 0.825, 95% CI 0.816-0.834; DeLong &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;&amp;lt;.001; positive NRI). Critically, even after removing the age-predictable component, age-residualized embedding retained modest but reproducible discrimination in both internal (AUROC 0.590, 95% CI 0.581-0.599) and external (AUROC 0.579, 95% CI 0.567-0.592) validation (both &lt;i&gt;P&lt;/i&gt;&amp;lt;.001), indicating dementia-relevant information that was not explained by chronological age. Of the 105 ECG labels, 19 (18.1%) showed a case-control difference that widened significantly toward diagnosis, dominated by nonspecific ST segment and T wave changes, acute ischemic patterns (acute myocardial infarction), and atrial and junctional arrhythmias (atrial flutter, ectopic atrial, and accelerated junctional rhythms), with divergence emerging mainly in the proximal (1-3-year) and imminent (0-1-year) windows. &lt;strong&gt;Conclusions:&lt;/strong&gt; Routine ECG foundation model embeddings encode aging-related electrophysiological information that is complementary to and not fully explained by chronological age and intensifies as diagnosis approaches, with concurrent shifts toward ischemic and arrhythmic ECG patterns whose mechanism cannot be determined from these data. These findings suggest that routine ECG is a low-cost, age-complementary triage signal for cognitive impairment and dementia screening, rather than a stand-alone diagnostic test. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/c94c2e86fb4c625255ceec775ac0be92" />

### [Evaluation of a Clinical Protocol–Guided Chatbot to Support Telecarers in Diabetes Management: Embedded Mixed Methods Study](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e91058)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `48` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e91058`

Background: Type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM) is a chronic condition that requires ongoing self-management. While chatbots offer scalable monitoring and management, evidence on their integration into structured hospital tele-support workflows, where patient engagement and digital readiness may differ from those in standalone digital interventions, remains limited. Objective: This study aimed to describe the design of a clinical protocol–guided chatbot, Sharing Information with Users on Diabetes Actions and Interventions (), and evaluate its feasibility, usability, and acceptability among patients and telecarers within a hospital tele-support program. Methods: We used an embedded mixed methods design (QUAL [quan]) guided by the World Health Organization (WHO) monitoring and evaluating digital health interventions and the theoretical framework of acceptability. Through a co-design process involving clinicians, researchers, telecarers, and developers, the chatbot was aligned with established clinical protocols. It featured 7 precall sessions designed to collect patient data, generate summary reports for staff, and deliver targeted educational content. Patients (≥21-year-old individuals with T2DM) and telecarer nurses (with experience ≥6 mo) were recruited from an existing tele-support program. Data sources included interviews, online surveys (5-point Likert scale), and chatbot usage logs. Qualitative data were analyzed thematically; quantitative data were summarized descriptively to supplement and contextualize the qualitative findings. Results: Fourteen participants (7 patients and 7 telecarers) completed both interviews and surveys and were included in the analysis. Patients (mean age 45.3, SD 15.4 y) had a median diabetes duration of 10 (IQR 3-25; range 1‐30) years, and all were digitally literate. Telecarers had a median of 5 (IQR 4-9) years of telehealth experience. Quantitatively, patients showed high engagement, completing 79% of started sessions with a median completion time of 2 minutes and 58 seconds. Patient satisfaction was high, with 86% (6/7) willing to recommend the tool; however, telecarer endorsement for universal rollout to patients in other clinical programs or outpatient clinics was more cautious (3/7, 43%). Qualitative findings revealed 5 themes: (1) perceptions of chatbot usability, (2) alignment and integration into telecarers’ processes, (3) factors influencing acceptability of the chatbot, (4) perceived efficacy of the chatbot for T2DM self-management and care delivery, and (5) suggested improvements and enhancements. Integrated findings revealed that the chatbot was usable and functioned as a precall support tool that optimized the personalization of tele-support sessions. However, its perceived efficacy in self-management behavior was limited by this cohort’s extensive experience with T2DM management. Key barriers to adoption included administrative burden for telecarers due to a lack of electronic medical record integration and content redundancy for patients. Suggested enhancements included multilingual support and adaptive content catering to different levels of disease-specific literacy. Conclusions: A clinical protocol–guided chatbot is feasible for hospital tele-support, complementing rather than replacing human care. It automates routine assessments and reinforces education, but future iterations must prioritize workflow integration and adaptive content to sustain engagement. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/3878e98564a88d63742978bdd42f79ab" />

### [Explainable Machine Learning for Predicting Major Adverse Renal and Cardiovascular Events in Acute Myocardial Infarction With Chronic Kidney Disease: Algorithm Development and Validation](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96654)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `52` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e96654`

Background: Major adverse renal and cardiovascular events (MARCE) have been proposed as a comprehensive end point reflecting both cardiac and renal outcomes in patients with acute myocardial infarction (AMI) and chronic kidney disease (CKD). However, existing prediction tools lack accuracy and generalizability for this high-risk population. Objective: This study aimed to develop and externally validate explainable machine learning models and a nomogram for predicting in-hospital MARCE among patients with AMI and CKD. Methods: We enrolled patients with AMI and CKD who underwent emergency treatment at the First Affiliated Hospital of Wenzhou Medical University as the discovery cohort and those at Wenzhou TCM (traditional Chinese medicine) Hospital of Zhejiang Chinese Medical University as the external validation cohort. Clinical features, including demographic details, comorbidities, laboratory results, echocardiography measurements, procedural details, and medication parameters, were collected for all participants. Important variables were selected using least absolute shrinkage and selection operator regression in the training set. We developed extreme gradient boosting (XGBoost), random forest, decision tree, and logistic regression (LR) models and evaluated their discrimination, calibration, and classification metrics in the hold-out test set and external cohort. Results: In the training set, we identified 6 important variables (Killip class, lactate, estimated glomerular filtration rate, use of diuretics, use of beta-blockers, and use of renin-angiotensin system inhibitors). In the test set, model discrimination was comparable across XGBoost (area under the receiver operating characteristic curve [AUC] 0.882, 95% CI 0.816-0.947), LR (AUC 0.875, 95% CI 0.799-0.950), and random forest (AUC 0.870, 95% CI 0.800-0.940), while decision tree showed lower performance (AUC 0.814, 95% CI 0.720-0.886). Shapley additive explanations (SHAP) values illustrated both global and individual feature contributions in the XGBoost model. A nomogram based on LR achieved an AUC of 0.873 (95% CI 0.714-0.926) in external validation. Conclusions: Our study identified 6 important predictors and provided a series of prediction models to enhance risk stratification of MARCE for patients with AMI and CKD. These models might aid in stratifying risk and choosing appropriate therapeutic strategies in the management of patients with AMI and CKD.

### [From simulation to pedagogy: multi-model and randomized evaluation of structured AI standardized patients](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03372-0)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `61` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03372-0`

Standardized patients (SPs) are central to clinical communication training but remain constrained by cost, scalability, and reliance on trained actors. We present AI standardized patients (AI-SPs), large language model–driven patient simulators built on a three-layer information architecture that adaptively modulates information disclosure according to learner skill. In Phase 1, blinded expert assessment of 350 simulated consultations generated by five frontier LLMs showed that, under a common architecture, learner skill level accounted for more of the observed performance variation than model choice (η2&nbsp;=&nbsp;0.31 vs. 0.06); whether this reflects the architecture itself cannot be determined without comparing models with and without it. In Phase 1b, analysis of 155 live student consultations identified systematic failures to elicit safety-critical information, enabling automated curriculum diagnostics without expert observation. In a Phase 2 three-arm pilot randomized study (n&nbsp;=&nbsp;58), OSCE performance gains with AI-SP training did not differ significantly from those with human SP training; the trial was designed to estimate effect sizes for a future confirmatory study, not to demonstrate equivalence. Self-efficacy gains were numerically largest with AI-SP training, but this exploratory difference did not survive correction for multiple comparisons. These findings suggest that architecture-driven AI-SPs could provide a scalable, model-portable approach to clinical communication training.

### [Gradient-driven microfluidic framework for the screening of ratiometric drug combinations towards precision oncology in paediatric leukaemia](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01818-0)

- Source: `nature_biomedical_engineering` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `57` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01818-0`

Improving outcomes for high-risk acute lymphoblastic leukaemia (ALL) patients requires increasingly personalized treatment strategies that leverage molecular and omics-based assays as well as functional screens. However, to date, only a few of these approaches are clinically actionable and easily adopted in clinical settings. To that end, we report a combination drug screening assay (GradientScreen) that correlates with clinical outcomes using standard paediatric ALL treatment protocols. This assay uses a microfluidic device to generate overlapping drug gradients and implements an analysis framework that characterizes drug synergy and efficacy in a ratiometric manner. Using partial least squares regression, we show that our combination response metrics correlate with both minimal residual disease (MRD) following initiation of treatment and later clinical end-points. Here, we further demonstrate that this system could be used to evaluate response to experimental therapeutic regimens in patients who did not respond to standard treatment. This assay can serve as a research-enabling tool for the field, with potential to integrate into clinical pathology workflows to identify difficult-to-treat ALL patients and screen for alternative treatment strategies at diagnosis. A gradient-based microfluidic framework analyses the functional responses of leukaemia cells from paediatric ALL patients to ratiometric combinations of three candidate drugs.

### [Hydrogel for sustained and localized delivery of a regulatory T cell-inducing agent promotes bone regeneration](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01817-1)

- Source: `nature_biomedical_engineering` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `61` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41551-026-01817-1`

Local activation of regulatory T (Treg) cells represents a promising strategy for wound repair and tissue regeneration. However, current Treg-based therapies rely on adoptive cell transfer, which requires ex vivo expansion and reinfusion, posing challenges to donor variability and tissue specificity. Here we engineer a hydrogel-based system for in situ Treg activation at injury sites through the controlled release of AS2863619 (AS), a cyclin-dependent kinase inhibitor. AS is incorporated into collagen hydrogels at concentrations exceeding its solubility limit, reducing burst release and extending the release half-life. This continuous exposure to Treg-inducing agents stabilizes Foxp3 expression in CD4+ T cells, thereby enabling Treg induction and stabilization. The incorporation of laponite nanoparticles further slows AS release and prolongs drug retention. In a calvarial bone-defect model, AS-loaded hydrogels induce dose-dependent Treg cell activation, reduce pro-inflammatory macrophage responses and enhance bone regeneration. Notably, AS–laponite hydrogels achieve complete defect bridging in some cases. By providing spatiotemporal control over Treg populations without the need for cell isolation and expansion, this approach offers a scalable alternative for immunomodulation and tissue repair. A laponite nanoparticles-based hydrogel loaded with a cyclin-dependent kinase inhibitor induces localized regulatory T (Treg) cell activation directly in vivo and enhances bone regeneration in a calvarial bone-defect mouse model.

### [Screening for Cough Variant Asthma Using the TabPFN Model on Routine Laboratory Data in a Multicenter Cohort: Prospective Validation Study](https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e92211)

- Source: `jmir_medinform` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `55` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://medinform.jmir.org/2026/1/e92211`

<strong>Background:</strong> Cough variant asthma (CVA) is a prevalent but underdiagnosed subtype of chronic cough, largely due to the limited accessibility of pulmonary function tests in primary care. <strong>Objective:</strong> This study aimed to develop and prospectively validate an interpretable machine learning model using routine laboratory data to facilitate accessible CVA screening. <strong>Methods:</strong> We conducted a multicenter study combining retrospective model development and prospective validation using data from January 2021 to August 2025. The study included 3 sets—a retrospective development set (n=533; 428 training and 105 internal testing) and 2 prospective validation sets (internal validation: n=59 and external validation: n=78). In total, 54 routine biochemical, coagulation, and hematological markers were analyzed. A Tabular Prior-Data Fitted Network (TabPFN) model was developed and benchmarked against extreme gradient boosting (XGBoost). Performance was evaluated via area under the curve (AUC), accuracy, and specificity. Shapley Additive Explanations (SHAP) and decision curve analysis (DCA) were used to assess interpretability and clinical utility. <strong>Results:</strong> In external validation, TabPFN achieved an AUC of 0.858 (95% CI 0.763-0.943), accuracy of 79.5%, sensitivity of 71.4%, and specificity of 86%. XGBoost achieved a higher external AUC of 0.912 (95% CI 0.842-0.982) and sensitivity of 91.4%, but lower accuracy (75.6%) and specificity (62.8%) at the fixed 0.5 probability threshold. TabPFN retained higher AUCs than XGBoost in internal testing (0.964 vs 0.890) and temporal validation (0.925 vs 0.846). DCA demonstrated that TabPFN provided higher net benefit across clinically relevant threshold probabilities, with the range varying across validation cohorts. SHAP analysis identified serum sodium (Na) and prothrombin time international normalized ratio as the leading predictors, followed jointly by mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration and red cell distribution width-SD, which showed the same rounded mean absolute SHAP value at the reported 2-decimal precision, and elevated sodium levels (&gt;141 mmol/L) were associated with increased predicted risk. <strong>Conclusions:</strong> The TabPFN model demonstrated robust prospective performance as an accessible screening tool for CVA, with balanced threshold-dependent classification performance across validation cohorts. Although TabPFN showed higher discrimination than XGBoost in internal and temporal validation, XGBoost achieved a higher AUC in the external cohort, indicating that neither model uniformly outperformed the other across all performance dimensions.

### [Technology-based structural racism and telehealth video usage among primary care patients](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03342-6)

- Source: `npj_digital_medicine` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `63` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41746-026-03342-6`

We tested whether technology-based structural racism mechanisms (digital redlining, path dependency, technical difficulties) were associated with lower telehealth video visit rates (versus audio-only; in-person visits excluded) among Black compared to White patients. Using structured and unstructured electronic health record data from 137,297 patients with completed telehealth visits over 26.5 months linked with broadband market data, we examined correlates of racial differences in video versus audio-only visits. Patient race had no significant unadjusted effect, but adjustment for individual and zip code level confounders revealed a 6% reduction in video visit rate ratios for Black patients, widening to 7–9% with interaction terms. For digital redlining, broadband market conditions were worse in areas with high neighborhood disadvantage and Black residential composition. However, broadband conditions were not associated with video visit rates. For path dependency, portal nonenrollment was more common among Black patients but had a stronger negative association with video visit rates among White patients. For technical difficulties, Black patients experienced more difficulties, which were associated with greater odds of a subsequent audio-only visit—particularly among Black patients. Findings suggest technology-based structural racism may operate through conditional and sequential mechanisms across telehealth stages. Health systems should monitor multilevel telehealth inequities across care stages.

### [Temporal Stability of Repeated Self-Reported vs Device-Measured Behavior: Observational Study Using Ecological Momentary Assessment](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e99953)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e99953`

Background: A central concern in ecological momentary assessment (EMA) research is whether repeated self-report alters the reporting process itself. In most EMA domains, longitudinal stability of reporting is assumed but rarely tested. Objective: This study leveraged concurrent accelerometry as a temporal anchor to evaluate (1) whether self-reported physical activity (PA) and sedentary behavior (SB) exhibited temporal shifts across a 7-day monitoring period and (2) whether this temporal shift differed across near-momentary (5-min reporting period) and coverage-based (120-min recall period) EMA protocols. Methods: We conducted a secondary analysis of 315 US adults recruited from the Understanding America Study panel between January and July 2023 for the current study. Participants were monitored in their natural environments for 7 days via 2 measurement methods: continuous thigh-worn activPAL4 accelerometry and 5 daily smartphone EMA prompts assessing PA and SB. Reporting reactivity was defined as divergence in the temporal trajectories of EMA-reported behavior relative to accelerometry. Multilevel models with random intercepts and slopes tested whether linear and quadratic study day effects differed across the 2 methods (method × study day interactions). Protocol differences were examined using multivariable method × protocol × study day interactions, comparing coverage-based and near-momentary EMA designs. Results: In the coverage-based protocol, EMA-reported PA and SB exhibited temporal trajectories that diverged significantly from concurrent accelerometry (SB linear divergence: =1.40, 95% CI 0.20-2.61, =.02; PA linear divergence: =−1.91, 95% CI −2.93 to −0.90, &lt;.001; and PA quadratic divergence: =0.28, 95% CI 0.12-0.44, &lt;.001). No evidence of such divergence was found in the near-momentary protocol. The divergence in reporting trajectories differed significantly between the 2 protocols (SB linear 3-way interaction: =−2.58, =.02; PA linear 3-way interaction: =1.86, =.04). Conclusions: Reporting reactivity in EMA appears protocol-dependent. The coverage-based protocol was associated with modest reporting reactivity, which challenges the common assumption of reporting invariance in EMA and underscores the importance of considering protocol design when interpreting within-person change. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/9df76ac83bb6f015bc8a9c3056a88675" />

### [The Ethics of Autonomous Clinical AI: Shifting From Model Accuracy to Model Impact](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e112246)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `56` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e112246`

The ethics of autonomous clinical AI are controversial. Traditional ethics assessments have focused on AI model performance rather than health system outcomes. Nichol and colleagues translate established research ethics principles into 9 practical recommendations for trials of autonomous clinical AI. Their findings support an evolution in clinical trial design. The accuracy and performance of the model is important, but the ethical unit of evaluation should be the complete care pathway, with prespecified obligations for subgroup performance, follow-up care, system failures, and model change. <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/4c8f52b76f61ddbadf7552e03b0a60e3" />

### [The Impact of Telemedicine Interventions on Maternal and Fetal Outcomes in Women With Gestational Diabetes: Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials](https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e103569)

- Source: `jmir` · published `2026-10-23` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `62` · status: `scored`
- Event: `seen` · identity: `url:https://www.jmir.org/article/view/jmir_v28i1e103569`

Background: Gestational diabetes mellitus (GDM) has a rising global prevalence and brings short-term and long-term adverse risks for mothers and fetuses. Conventional in-person GDM care is limited by time and geographic barriers, often resulting in suboptimal compliance and delayed individualized interventions. Telemedicine is increasingly applied in GDM management; however, randomized controlled trial (RCT) evidence remains inconsistent. Objective: The study aims to pool data from RCTs to quantify the effects of telemedicine on glycemic control and pregnancy-related outcomes in women with GDM and to inform optimized clinical management. Methods: We searched PubMed, Embase, Cochrane Library, Web of Science, and Scopus up to August 16, 2026. Two reviewers independently performed study screening and data extraction. Risk of bias was assessed using RoB 2. Meta-analyses were conducted in Stata 18.0. For heterogeneous outcomes, 95% prediction intervals (PIs) were calculated to estimate effect ranges for future studies. Heterogeneity was explored via leave-one-out sensitivity, meta-regression, and subgroup analyses. The GRADE (Grading of Recommendations Assessment, Development, and Evaluation) framework rated evidence certainty. Publication bias was evaluated for outcomes reported in ≥10 studies. Results: Twenty-three RCTs (3055 women with GDM) were included. Pooled estimates suggested that telemedicine may reduce fasting glucose (mean difference [MD] −0.26, 95% CI −0.42 to −0.09, 95% PI −0.93 to 0.42; =.002), 2-hour postprandial glucose (MD −0.50, 95% CI −0.87 to −0.14, 95% PI −1.84 to 0.84; =.007), and gestational weight gain (MD −0.78, 95% CI −1.40 to −0.17, 95% PI −2.83 to 1.26; =.01). Telemedicine was also associated with lower risks of cesarean section, emergency cesarean section, macrosomia, neonatal hypoglycemia, and neonatal respiratory distress. No significant differences were found for hemoglobin A, preterm birth, or neonatal intensive care unit admission. Notably, fasting glucose, 2-hour postprandial glucose, and gestational weight gain exhibited very high unexplained heterogeneity (²&gt;90%), with their 95% PIs crossing the null value despite statistically significant pooled effects. The GRADE certainty of evidence was very low to low across all assessed outcomes. Conclusions: Telemedicine may improve selected glycemic and perinatal outcomes for women with GDM and may serve as a promising supplementary option for prenatal care, particularly in resource-limited settings where routine in-person follow-up is difficult to implement. Nevertheless, the apparent benefits for glycemic control and gestational weight gain remain tentative due to extreme, unexplained between-study heterogeneity and 95% PIs crossing the null. Based on current evidence, telemedicine should not completely replace standard antenatal care. Future well-designed, large-sample RCTs are warranted; such trials should adopt standardized definitions for telemedicine interventions, fully report intervention components, and conduct long-term follow-up to verify sustained maternal and neonatal clinical benefits. Trial Registration: PROSPERO CRD420261403669; https://www.crd.york.ac.uk/PROSPERO/view/CRD420261403669 <img src="https://jmir-production.s3.us-east-2.amazonaws.com/thumbs/94f0f71111a8230fc759f1db91f598d2" />

### [Neoadjuvant nivolumab with or without visugromab in muscle-invasive bladder cancer: the phase 2a GDFATHER-NEO trial](https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04740-9)

- Source: `nature_medicine` · published `2026-10-24` · freshness `fresh`
- Reading value: `65` · status: `scored`
- Event: `new` · identity: `url:https://www.nature.com/articles/s41591-026-04740-9`

Growth differentiation factor-15 (GDF-15) is overexpressed in muscle-invasive bladder cancer (MIBC) and was recently identified as a potential therapeutic target to overcome resistance to programmed cell death protein 1/programmed death ligand 1 (PDL1) blockade. We investigated the safety and efficacy of GDF-15 blockade in an exploratory, stratified, placebo-controlled, single-blinded, nonrandomized phase 2a trial (GDFATHER-NEO). Thirty-one cisplatin-ineligible or cisplatin-refusing patients with newly diagnosed MIBC (clinical T2–T4aN0M0) were stratified by tumor stage and PDL1 combined positive score (CPS) and allocated sequentially to receive three cycles of neoadjuvant nivolumab (N, 480 mg 4-weekly) plus the GDF-15-neutralizing antibody visugromab (V, 20 mg kg−1 4-weekly; n = 16) or placebo (P, n = 15), followed by radical cystectomy or repeat transurethral resection of the bladder tumor. Locally assessed pathologic complete response (pCR, ypT0N0/x) and Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors v1.1-based radiographic response served as dual primary endpoints. pCR was 33% (5 of 15, 95% confidence interval (CI) 12–62%) in patients receiving N + V versus 8% (1 of 13, 95% CI 0–36%) for N + P; major pathologic response was 60% (95% CI 32–84%) versus 23% (95% CI 5–54%), and radiographic overall response rate was 60% (95% CI 32–84%) versus 15% (95% CI 2–45%), respectively. Responses to N + V were associated with higher baseline densities of total and proliferating (Ki-67+) CD8+ T cells, but independent of CPS and baseline GDF-15 levels in serum and tumor. There were no treatment-related discontinuations or treatment-related delays to surgery; grade 3–4 treatment-related adverse events occurred in two (13%) and three (20%) patients treated with N + V or N + P, respectively. These findings support further evaluation of visugromab in MIBC. ClinicalTrials.gov identifier: NCT06059547 . As presented at the ESMO Congress 2026: In this exploratory, nonrandomized phase 2a trial, treatment of patients with immune checkpoint inhibitor-naive muscle-invasive bladder cancer with the GDF-15-neutralizing antibody visugromab and nivolumab produced encouraging pathological complete response and radiographic response rates with no new safety signals, supporting further evaluation in larger randomized studies.
